COUNTY OF CHESTER. WEIGHT AND MEASURES ACT. BYE-LAWS REGULATING THE SALE OF COAL.

NTOTICE is HEREBY GIVEN, that the following BYE-LAWS regulating toe Sale of Coal withi. the Dietrict under tee jurisdiction of the County Council of the County of Chestec were adopted at a Quarterly Meeting of the said Council, held at Chester Castle, on Friday, the 10th day et NOVENIBER,IB99, and approved by the Board of Trade on the 28th cay of December 1899, and therefore now in operation, viz :- I.—Every person upon whose behalf Coal, in quantities not exceeding two hundred-weight, is hawked, sold or exposed. for sale by retail, shall cause his name and place of hilliness to be registered at the Head Office of the Weights and Measures Department of the County Council at Chester Castle, and on each and every occasien on which he shall, change his registered place of business he shall, within four- teen days thereafter, cause to be communicated to the said office particulars of such change. 2.—Every person whe hall sell or offer for sale or de- liver to a purchaser or shall cause to be sold sir offered for sale or delivered to a purchaser, from any vehicle, hose, or ether beast of burden, Coal in quantities not exceeding two hundred-weight shall carry, or cause to be carried, such Coal in sacks, bags or other receptacles containing either two hundred-weight, one hundred-weig ht, half a hundred-weight, or a quarter of a hundred- weight ; and the top of every such sack, bag, or otber receptacle, shall 'have permanently fastened to its metal label indicating the correct weight of Coal therein ; and no person shall sell or offer for sale, or deliver to a purchaser. or ellen cause to be sold or offered for sale or delivered to a purchaser, Coal which is of leas weight than that specified on the label attached to such sack, bag, or other receptacle. 3.—Every person who shall sell or offer for sale, or de- liver to a purchaser or shall cause to be sold or offered for sale or delivered to a purchaser, Coal in sacks, bags, or other receptacles, from any vehicle, horse or other beast of bureen, in or on which Coal is carried for sale or delivery to more than one pur- chaser, shall carry or cause to be carried therewith, a correct weighing iratrument of a form approved by the Local Authority, and duly stamped by an Inspector for thi- County, together with the neces- sary correctlweigiats,and the person in charge of such vehicle, horse, or other beast of burden, shall re- weigh the Coal upon being requested to do so by the purchaser, or by anyone on behalf of the purchaser, or by an Inspector of Weights and Measures. 4—Every person who shall sell or offer for sale, or de- liver to a purchaser, or cause to be sold or offered for sale or delivered to a purchaser, from any vehicle, Coal in quantities not exceeding two hundred-weight shall have clearly and leeibly painted upon a conspicuous part of the right band side, or of the front, of such vehicle, in letters not less than one inch in height and of proportionate breadth, the name and place of business of the person on whose behalf such Celle is sold or offered for sale or delivered to a purchaser, and shall keep the same legible and undefeced. s,—Every persen who shall sell, or cause to be sold, at any waarf, yard, or othar place, Coal in quantities not exceeding two huedred-weight, shall sell, or cause to be sold, such Coal in quantities of either two hundred-weight, one hundred-weight, half a hundred-weight, or a varter of a hundred-weight, and shall weigh, or cause to be weighed, such coal by means of a correct weighing instrument duly verified and stamped by an Inspector ot Weights and Measures for this County, and if required, shall re-weigh, or came to be re-weighed, the same in the presence of the purchaser, or of any one on behalf of the purchaser, or of an Inspector of Weights and Measures . 6.—Every person who shall s,ll or deliver to a purchaser, or shall cause to be sold or delivered to a purchaser, Coal frcua a vehicle, shill carry such Coal for the purpose of being weighed or re-weighed any die. tar ce not exceeding half a mile, as may he required by an Inspector of Weights and Measures. 7.—The penalty of every breach, contravention, or non- ebaerae ot any one or any part of any one of the foregoioe Bye-laws shill be any sum not exceeding five pounds. - B,—From and after the date of the confirmation of these Rye-laws the Bye-laws which were made by the County Council on the sth day of February, 1891, and confirmed by the Board of Trade on the 24th day of April, 1891, shall be repealed. LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE, The weather hao. been 60 bad during the past week that Outdoor costumes are nob of the most elaborate ,character. Our attention naturally turns to indoor dresses, and that ever-fresh subject, blouses, receives daily, notice. Evening parties make pretty blouses indispensable, and many thoughts ara directed to the choice of them. Some are in dainty spotted net, white or pale- tinted, and some of them ara showered with steel or silver sequins. Some lovely lace is occasionally introduced. A large flower Luay be worn on one shoulder, the buds drooping towards the waist. Very pretty bodices have a square finish, with a broad band of open work, jetted or jewelled work. The trimming is cenninued down the front of the dress, which is in satin striped with palest green. A wide black. velvet band forms a necklet. The sleeves consist of a network of narrow black velvet, into which a band of the jewelled trimming is fastened, a frill of pleated chiffon 83f toning thr hard line round tho arms.

The hat remaing pretty much the mite. There are many toques about. Recently a toque I saw had the crown in citron cloth, embroidered in jet and shaded si)km, from orange down to the deepest tint of brown in sable, .the brim beiog in the fur, and a wide flat bow of orange velvet armngei just above it in the centre, with a large jet buckle for the centre. Another hat was ia brown velvet, with shaded plume, illnstrating the very wide toque, with upstanding feathere, sometimes rather crazy-looking. Aeoth,..r hat was a theroTply seasonable naodel. The brim was a brown felt one, moderate in dimensions and somewhat cur- tailed at the back a la mode, though not exces- sively so. The crown was of sable, and round it was a folded scarf of eatin merv, shading from a chrysanthemum pink to the red of' the same flowers, fastened up on the left side by a very smut buckle.

Now there is one point I want to be geneially )loserved. it is that blouses must be of such a thickness as to meet all changes of the weather, silk is all inadequate; a blouse in fancy crepon or other woollen fabric; would be better suited. Velveteen is offered as being warmer than silk or surah ; but, though it feels thicker, it is actually oolder than these. Panne is the favourite material for the blouse, and some are made of cloth, strapped and stitched with panne. In all the new blouse bodices there is the slight pouch effect at the waist to which we have row become accustomed, and which locks so smart. The only way to render' these useful for day wear in very cold weather is to have them interlined with flannel or merino ; and, as this adds to the bulk, it would not be a very popular plan. The spotted panne is much liked, and blouses made of it are often strapped with plain panne. The sleeve I saw the other day on one blouse had thirty-six strappings of panne laid on round the arm; The whole of the bodice was strapped longitu&sally, so the amount of work may be dividee,

The cold has given rise to the very comfortable little fashion of 'A e&ring a eille or chiffon tie round the base of the stem collar, end thereby pressing It in closely and protectively round the neck. This Is by no means unneceseary, for the storm collar has a mean way of ',Admitting cold air at the back, which is decidedly reprehensible. The fur boleros that stop some inches above the waist are dangerous garments leaving exposed the base of the lungs. The very long coats are not sensible wear, since they provide vrs.tenth where it is not needed, over a multiplicity of warm eleirt4 and woven under- wear. But they are bril!.:an:.ly practical, as corn. pared with the abbreviieed bolero, cut away in order that thc.tslirmiess of the waist may not be hidden. Infl ienz I. is busy among us, and this bolero is Orie of his an:lye coadjutors. The other afternoon I noticed a very pretty cloth gown, the shade being dark copper colour. The skirt had two wi,ie pleats at the back, and round the base were eleven rows of narrow gold pion. The bodice was plain at the back, and the front opened over a white satin waistcoat covered with Venetian lace and a jabot and collar of copper-coloured satin ribbon with tiny gold galon sewn cn the satin. The sleeves were plaia with lace at the wrists.

Your loving, JENNIII.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Dr.' sacs for the early Drawing Rooms are in contempla- tion indeed many are already in course of conetruction. S.) far nothing very strikingly new has been allowed to appear either in fabric or style, but the modistes have probably many little surprises for us later on. Despite this dreadful wer, a brilliant season is autieepated. To le.?eir. with, he Princess of Wales e ill once again take her acetustemost place at all Reyal functiecs,the Duchess of York is also expected to appi ar, and an Jain there is nothing to prevent a goodly number of Her Majesey's descendants and connections, bents.; en evidence both in the Throne Room. atai Ball Room, at Buckingham Palace this cement,. season. Miss Sheelith Corms-al:is West, whose efforts for the success of her concert—in aid of the War Fund—have been so untiring, and so tmieently satisfactory, though scarcely so lovely as her sister Princess Beery of Pleas, is an extremely pretty girl. Her clerk hair, dark eyes, and rich colettrine, reproduce much ot the brilliant beauty of her charming mother. Considers be interest wee excited gime -months ago, by the rumour that a merria,ee hag bsen arranged between Miss West and the then Lord B-lgrave, now Duke of Wesrminster, but nothing further has been beard of any such arrangement. The Duke, who c erhes of age this year, is fairly good looking, and poetesses the great advantage of a charm- ing manner. The ciiiputatiens over the question whether the new century begins this year or Deer, have given place to a fresh centrovsray, with reeard to ahnTevilthg the date " 1.900." Soniet advocate 'OO, and seine 19—, and others say neither is a proper abbreviation and that none is p -.ssible. The Post Office, however, have so far settled the question that Linty are using 'OO on their date stamps, and this will no doubt establish the custom. Unfortunately there seems to bo no such cleciAve authority to settle the century question, which hate separated whole families, and life-long friends, be caus- ing embittered arguments and quarrels. It is surprising how little suffiere to make some people lose their tempers and quarrel with all the force of deadly enmity. - There is, far instance an eld-staniling quarrel between two wellsknown regiments in the British Army, which all arorre over the question of the respective merits of the regimental Christmas dinner, many years ago. It eventuellv led to one of the worst Aldershot fights toe record. That was in the old days of the Duke of Cam- bridge, who had the regiments out, and told them he could rot send them to the place he would like to, but he could send them abroad, and away they soon went on three years foreign service. Still, the quest -n has never been settled, and it is saidethat even now the War Office dare net send the to regiments to the same theatre of war, for fear that the old standing feud should break cut again, even in front of the enemy. An American claims to be able to tell a man's charac- ter by the way in which he wears his hat. As a science it seems to have lese to recommend it than other me- diums of fertune telling, such as palmittry,handwritieg, and the soles of one's beets and so on. Still, we are told, that the man who has it pushed back is frank and joviel. Those with hats too small and worn jauntily on one side, belong to the vain and foppish class tt hile the man with a hat too largo and down over his ears, is a philosopher, or at least belongs to the reflective sort. Taere ie perhaps some truth in this, but no more than is known to every ordinary obs ,rver. A much better test of character than shape and style, is whether one's hat fits oe not, and it is just the EMMA with boots and ol her gaimente. The weak and caciilating will often be palmed off with ill-fitttne articles, whereas the strong minded will only have what they wart, and what they consider a suitable fit. At the shops whore "Sales "are in full swing one and I eleven pence three farthines seems to he the price of any garment that is not a coat and skirt—that is a little more expensive. A pair of corsets, a pair of gloves, a toque. a eair of shoes, a blouse, etc, etc., all these are en evidence at the old familiar one and eleverspence three larthings. Personally " Sales "are an investment I seldom dabble in. There is too much risk about it. When all the brilliant tickets bills, and rnasses of divers articles have uisappeared, the windows have been polished up and duly set out with new season's goods, then I sally forth, and if one gets less for one's money as regards quantity it is more than probable that one gains as re- gards quality and style. What is the use for example, of a costume which clearly bears the stamp of 1899, however extensively marked "down "? What is the use of gloves already soiled, and that don't fit ; of corsets that break, while being "tried on"; of shoes that burst after one or two days wear; of a blouse impossible in fit and style, of headgear crushed, and already out of date, of coats and skirts bearing plainly the hall mark"made in Germany?" ' There are occasional bareains to be had undoubtedly, but taking into consideration the time and strength necessary to secure them, they always seem to me dear at the price. Gloves are of the palest possible tints such as mastic putty. biscuit, etc., and suede are considered smarter than kid. Four buttons are the correct length for all day purposes, while for evening it hovers between this, and shoulder length. Transparent lace sleeves are fre• quently carried over the hand mitten fashiotaand gloves cli‘pensed with alicesether, except for dancing, and this fashion will doubtless recommend itself to the impecuni- ous to wham the long glove is a serious item. Mud, everywhere mud ! Lately the three things one has needed most are—the patience of Jobea stout clothes brush, and a spacious garden. It is of iso use attempt- ing to hush a walking skirt that has done duty on such days as we have lately experienced with a weak bristled brush, or within the compass of four walls, neither one's temper or the family abode would be improved thereby. Waterproof coats have been well to the fore, and de- lightfully faseinatine many of them are. The best, are the " Cravenette " These are rain proof, at the same time porous and perfectly ventilated. We seem, as a nation to consume a good deal of tea, the total for last year being 245 million• pounds, or an increase of ten million pounds on the year befcre. In adlition to the normal increase of population, we have the fact that last year was a particularly prospereus one for the country,and no doubt a Rood deal of the increase is due to that circumstance. If may be that another factor in the eine is the wastefulneFs of many pecple who make tea, the idea which possess not a few houne- wives being that the way to make good tea, is to make it as strong as possible.

TUE EXAMINER,

(Copyright.)

NOBLER THAN REVENGE.

A By ESME STUART,

Author of "In the Dark," "Inscrutable," "A Woman of Forty," "Sara's Mother," Scc.

CHAPTER XVIII.

When Alec again returned to the consciousness of his own, identity, the pangs of hunger were diminished; but now, what was much worse, his tongue seemed to be too big for his mouth, his throat was parched, and his lips swollen. To die of thirst is far worse than to die of hunger. In this close, airless closet the horror of dying by inch** was far worse than any sudden death could be. It was the realisation of this that at last made Alec Beauworth struggle to his feet, determined to make one last effort for his life. He had knocked in vain , at the doors, but now he thought that the trap-door , through which he had been precipitated was, after all, the floor of his bedroom. His one chance must be to make himself heard in that direction. Of course, if the Feltons were at Yewdale, they would take care that no sound should reach the ears of the servants; but he knew that they must now have left the house, for Ruby would have to be hurried away as soon as possible to get her out of his way. The very idea of Ruby gave him courage. Oh, to be , free and to take full vengeance for all the wrongs , she had endured! And added to hers would be his own. Surely the law would help him. He would I hunt down James Felton and the squire to the ends I of the earth. He would unmask them, for Sir Harry was unworthy the name of Beauworth. Then came a terrible thought. How could he prove that his fall had been contrived purposely? A man like James Felton is clever enough to evade the law. There was no witness of their interview. "0 God, give me life and give me vengeance!" thought Alec, dumbly sending up this prayer from the depth of his heart. "Vengeance is Mine "—the answer seemed to come to his mind as if the words were spoken in his ear. He staggered to his feet and looked around. He had now got together everything that could raise him up high enough to Ireach the ceiling. Happily for him, the rubbish included several old chairs. One was too broken to be of any use except as ti prop. With the help of two others, he managed to make a firm foundation. Then, by very slow degrees, he heaped on this all he could pick up, and so made a firm footing; but he had to stop between each effort so as to gain strength for the next. When his 'pile was raised, he had to rest before making a great trial of climbing on to his erection. He could not then reach the ceiling with his hand, and he had again to grope about for further help. At last he found part of an old billiard cue, and after another rest, he carefully mounted his scaffold. He knew that if it fell he might not have sufficient energy to' re- build it. Indeed, the whole thing seemed to be a forlorn hope, but still he was determined to do all he could to save his life, for did not his life mean the possibility of doing something in the future for Ruby, though, alas! he felt sure that he should be too late to prevent the marriage. She could never have found the means of 'escape. Very carefully he mounted on his unstable plat- form, and at last he just reached the ceiling with his cue. His strength was not great, but he husbanded it, knocking again and again. He knocked till he was too weary to hold the cue, and , once more he had to rest. He must certainly have made very audible sounds, for the old rafters re- echoed the knocks as the dull thud reverberated through the beams. Again he rested,' then he began again. Someone might come to open or shut the windows upstairs. The room, too; must be swept out, and if it were not already done there was Icertainly some chance of his being heard. Once he femme* he distinguished a sound above, and hastily began again to knock as loud as he could. He Icould hardly hold the cue. He felt that he must soon give up all effort, for the agony of his thirst I was almost unbearable. Could he holdiout a little

longer? Suddenly he heard a thump on the floor above. IWas it in answer to him? He waited, fearing that this was a delusion, then again he raised his hand and knocked irregularly. Was there a sound? Yes, a very decided noise. He even seemed to hear a veice, but he could not be certain, though once more he answered, so as to make sure that he was not deluding himself. [Could it again be the faithful Bob? He certainly would have 'tried to find him, though, of course, the Feltons would have told lies about him. Hark! there was a sound, someone was using a tool. Ile must be careful that this was not the work of a foe, for even in this strange house nothing was safe. Alec was silent for a little time. The blood seemed to be rushing through his temples. It seemed to blind and to deafen him. Suppose he lost the power of answering. He might even now be overlooked. No, there was a sound, confused certainly, almost a whisper of: "Mr. Alec! Mr. Alec! Is that you ?" "Bob, Bob, a little water, for Heaven's sake. Can you open P" Another pause. Bob was trying to find the means to get at him. Never had a sound appeared so welcome to Alec as that shuffling noise, though evidently Bob was setting to work with great caution. This was, indeed, the case, and we must return to Bob after he had watched Sir Harry drive off in frantic haste. He had trembled for fear of being further questioned, but nothing had happened. Nancy was upstairs weeping and bemoaning her own and her darling's fate, for if Miss Ruby was not found, there was little chance of averting the storm that would burst upon her and upon her widowed daughter. • Old Rudd, from whom she sought consolation, was too busy to have much time for speculation. The Feltons had left everything in disorder, and the squire had given commands the evening before that all was to be replaced and the old rooms shut up again before his return. This might be soon, and Rudd tried to make Nancy bestir herself, and with the help of Jane, a strong girl, to set the house in order. But Nancy was of no use. She was really ill with worry; and the only thing to do was to send the girl to scrub the rooms which had been in use. Jane had begun downstairs, and not till these rooms and the passages were finished, did Rudd bethink himself that the best spare room had never been touched since Mr. Beauworth's sudden disappearance, and Jane was sent there. So it was Jane who first heard the dull thud, and, frightened out of her wits, she hurried out of the room to the hall downstairs. Going into the courtyard, she met Bob just returned from a flying visit to his mother. "Eh, Bob, look here, then; it's not me as is going into that room upstairs again. There's a ghost a-tapping there. I was scrubbing the sides of the floor and I heard it, and I'm that terrified that I dursn't go in. And there's my load there._ and if Nancy or Mr. Rudd find it not done—bless my stars! there'll be a dreful rumpus! What be I to do ? " Bob listened 'in silence. made up. "I heard there was a ghost there, Jane, and I don't wonder as you keep away." "What shall I do, Bob ? " "If you don't tell, Jane, I'll finish the scrubbing for you; but I must lock the door for fear the squire was to come back. Odds Dag! there would be a split if he was to find me doing your work!" "Bob, I'll do anything you like, if you'll do that for me. You are just obliging, that you are," "Where's the key ? " "Oh, it's in the door." "Then I'll take it out, and 'keep it till I've done; and if you tell on me, Jane, why, I'll—" "No fear, I'll not go there again, no, not if I was to be sent away. It gives me the death shivers, and that's the truth." Bob was soon up the stairs. The day was waning, so he took his stable lantern with him. He could hide the light should anyone watch him; but he knew that the squire being away, he was safe from intrusion. Rudd never did more than he could help. Bob entered softly. Taking out the key, he locked the door inside, so as to be sure he was not suddenly interrupted. He listened a little while, but he heard nothing. There was Jane's pail of water and the room still in a mess, just as it had been left the evening that Mr. Alec had departed. Bob searched the room again, looking iato every corner of the big Cupboard, which had before roused his suspicions. All was the same, and there was no noise. Suddenly he did hear a faint thud, but it seemed to come from below his feet. Bob started as if he had been shot. The floor! He had rimer thought of that. He listened again. Another knock. He seized Jane's broom, and gave an answering knock. If it was a human being he should get an answer. In another moment it was answered. Bob looked at the floor. There were large rugs covering the centre; near the dressing-table the edge was nailed down. He nulled at it, but it held fast. He looked. raujaci.for

Then his mind was

some means or rorcing out the nails. At last he twied a lucky horse-shoe, which some youthful Beauworth had once hung up over the doorway. Bob climbed up and unhooked it. Using it as a lever, he succeeded in lifting the carpet. He tore the coverings off, and there he saw disclosed the outline of a trap-door, but so well jointed that, without a clue, it would have been difficult to dis- cover the secret. He tried to lift it, calling as loud as he dared: "Mr. Alec! Mr. Alec ! " Yes, he heard an answer, he could almost distinguish the words, but not quite. He worked with a will; he must make haste. His friend must be almost dead. What if he had been nearly murdered and was dying below? If only he had some proper tools, he could have gone quicker • but he dared not go out of the room for fear of being questioned if seen coming out of this place. At last he was able to lift the door a very little, and then he distinctly heard the appeal for water. "Wait a minute, Mr. Alec; I'll get you some. Are you hurt ? " There was no answer this time, and Bob did not know how he was to manage, thinking "I was a blind fool not to guess he was not gone." He found a little water in a jug, but how was he to get it down? There was a water-bottle. He cut down the bell-rope as gently as he could, then be tied the bottle to it, and then tried once again to lift the heavy trap. He saw that the hinges had been lately replaced. This time be was more successful. He managed to get something under to hoist it an inch, then a bigger stool. At last he was able to hold the light. He saw that Mr. Alec was lying in a heap on a raised pile, but he did not move, and the cue which Irtd saved him had dropped from his hand, and lay where it had fallen on the floor. "Mr. Alec, for Heaven's sake, speak to me. It's Bob, 'tain't them deuced Feltons. Here is the water; I can't get down, at least not now. I shouldn't be able to get up again without a ladder, and you would be no better off." he cold bottle touched Alec's forehead, the slight shock revived him. "Water!" he said again. "Mr. Alec, just move a bit and take it. Here it is. You'll feel better after a drink. Here is the water. Just drink a drop. Oh, if I could hang them and chop them up into bits." Alec was roused by the word, "water " ; he collected his • wandering senses, and took hold of the bottle, but if Bob had not been holding it from above, he would not have had the strength to hold the precious bottle to his lips. As it was, he spilt a good deal, but a little trickled into his mouth, enough to make him sit up and take a firmer grasp. "Good Heavens, it's water!" he murmured. Bob watched him, and the tears trickled down his face.. No suffering of his own had ever drawn a tear from him. The fresh air was also beginning to do its work of reviving the prisoner. He was now able to drink a good draught, and then to ask for more. "Bob, you have saved me again! Give me some more water; I thought it was all over. If I die, don't let it be of thirst. Give me more." • Bob drew up the bottle, and refilled it again, but this .was all there was in' the room. "Bob, have you got anything to eat ? " "No, Mr. Alec, I ain't. So they meant to kill you. The devil was too sharp for me. It wants the devil to fight the devil!" "W.hat time is it? How long have I been here ? " "Don't rightly know; but let's get you out first. Can you help yourself at all ? " "I'm afraid I can't much." Bob looked round to see if he could find any- thing to help him. There were only the sheets and blankets on. the bed, but what would Nancy say if they were spoilt? He would be discoverell. Alec had now so far recovered that he could sit up, and he was as anxious to help Bob as Bob was to help him. "Bob, is there any way of getting out of this hole? There are two doors to open. "It would'nt be any good if I could find them even, Mr. Alec, I feel sure they would be well bolted." "I have •no strength to climb up there even

if-- "Never mind, we'll find a way. afraid of is the squire." "Don't run any risk.for me, my good Bob." " 'Tain't that, said Bob, scornfully, "but it's you, Mr. Alec. If the old gentleman comes home, why—" Alec wanted to ask a dozen questions, but he dared not, feeling that the little energy he still possessed must, be used. to help the faithful Bob. "If you think the doors are hopeless, I must try, with your help, to get out through the trap-door; •but I can find nothing more to help me." "If I could twist the sheets, I think that they would be able to bear you." "Yes, but could you hold me up ? " "We must try," said Bob. "Rest a bit, Mr. Alec, and I will get them ready. Can you tie a good knot rotted yourself ? " "I think I can do that, but I don't know what to say to the risk you are running." Bob went softly to the bed, and pulling off the sheets, he examined them. They were old linen sheets belonging to past generations of Beauworths. The sheets were to be trusted, but even so it was not easy. At last Bob had made his preparations, and he returned to his post. "Mr. Alec, I have fastened one end to the old bed-post, then I shall twist it again round the table. It's a heavy one, and I think that we can get you up. Do you feel better? I wish I had something to eat for you, but we had best get you up first. The devil's friends beat me, Mr. Alec. I kep an eye everywhere I thought, but it warn't enough." It took Alec a long time to make himself secure. He ,was very weak, and only his determination not to die in a hole like this made him pull himself together. "Bob, I've done it. Will it bear me? I feel that I can make very little effort." "Yes, Mr. Alec. - Now, you must just hold on; it's not far." This was true, but it was lifting almost a dead weight, and Bob was by no means sure of succeed- ing. At first it seemed impossible. If Alec could have helped himself more, he could have climbed up using it like a rope, ; but as Alec stood up he felt so giddy that he called out: "Stop, Bob, I don't think I shall be able to do this, and you will never be strong enough to pull me up by sheer force." • "Just try, Mr. Alec." For Bob's sake, Alec made another effort. Clinging to the impromptu rope, he wound his legs round the linen, and found that he was able to hoist himself an inch higher. "Hold tight, Bob. If you let go, it's all up with me." Bob knew it. He did not speak, but held on for dear life, the life of his friend. At last Alec's head approached the floor line, but now was the worst moment—he had not strength enough to cling to the edge. "Bob, I can't do any more. up a moment ? " Bob twisted himself round the sheet and lay down. Now he pulled with all his might, nearly squeezing the breath out of his body. "Mr. Alec, try to hoist a bit." One more effort, and Alec seized the edge. Bob pulled, then with a low exclamation, of pain, Bob saw that he had literally dragged the prisoner on to the floor of the bedroom. Both lay silent and quite exhausted for five minutes. Bob was the first to recover himself. He unwound himself, and now he could easily drag Alec into safety. Then he quickly closed the trap-door and replaced the carpets over it. "I thought for a moment we should have stuck in the middle, Mr. Alec," he said, smiling. Alec could just smile too as he held out his hand, but he was past words. "It's food you want, Mr. Alec • I'll just go and iset some. I'll lock you in, though." ." If they came now, I couldn't shew fight," whispered Alec. "No fear! They are gone to Australia, and I wish the squire had gone with them." Bob went softly out of the room, and locking it behind him, he put the key in his pocket. Downstairs be found Rudd talking to Nancy. sane was listening, open-mouthed. "Here it is, sure enough. It's from the squire." "What's -up now ? " said Bob, trying to look uninterested. "Wherever have you been, Bob ? " said Nancy. , Jane made faces to him from behind Nancy, to entreat him not to let out that he had been doing her work. "I was cleaning up. Squire coming home? " "Well, the squire says Miss Ruby is not found, and he'll come back to-morrow morning." Bob felt relieved. He feared the squire would have been back this evening. flo. reason for starving. I'm dead hungry, said Bob. "Well, sit down then," said Rudd, "We didn't' wait for you." "I didn't notice the time. The squire will be in a temper whenthe comes home About Miss Ruby"

When Bob slunk upstairs again he felt very much like a thief, but he managed to get into the room without being seen, and in another moment Alec was drinking a little brandy and eating a few mouthfuls of bread and butter.

"We have the night, Mr. Alec. The squire is not coming back here till to-morrow, but we must clear out early." "And has Miss Ruby sailed? " "No, that's the best of it. .r. Alec you'll never guess! why, she's cut and run. She's a Beaimorth, she is."

You see, I saw you were sweet on her, Mr. Alec, so I just gave her a telegram that came for you, and I took her to the train for to go to London. We were sharp." Bob laughel softly. "You ain't out of the wood, Mr. Alec." "No, but I mean to live and have my turn of revenge! If I could bring the squire to the gallows I would."

CHAPTER XIX.

When Ruby came to her senses she saw a sweet face bending over her. It was not the same face she had seen on entering the house, and so for some time she could not recollect where she was or what she could have been doing. "Papa," she said, softly. "Your father is not here, dear. Shall w 3 write to him ? " "Where am I ? " "With friends." "Alec's friend's? I was looking for them .when-" _ _

not Al's friends. Now try and sleep." "Oh, I must not; I must get up and look for Alec's friends.

"The doctor will not let you get up, dear. You have been very ill." "Have I?" She tried to sit up and fell back. "I see, Ican't sit up. Have I given you a great deal of trouble?"

• "Trouble! Are we not put into the world just to help our neighbours ? " "How good you are; but-"

.. There N '.V. as a sweet laugh. "Good! Oh, no, mother is longing to see you. I shall fetch her. We are sorry for you.".

"That was your mother I saii. I remember now. How long have I been here ? " "Never mind about dates. We wanted to write to your people, but you could not tell us." "Oh, lam glad. You must not write to any*. one. I will tell your mother." "You must not worry yourself. My brother will like to see you too, when you are better." "Was he the kind man who brought me here? Oh, I was so miserable." _ -

"Yes; he brought you here, I am glad to say." "Why?" "Don't talk any more now. I will give you your medicine, and when you wake up you will be better."

Ruby felt inclined to cry for joy. When next she woke she felt much better, and Ibis time it was the elder lady who sat in front of her. _ _ _

Ruby stretched out her hand. "Oh, you have been so kind to me." "It is a pleasure to help young people." "I was in such trouble. May I tell you? Only promise not to make me go back to him." "Tell me everything, dear." Ruby told her everything except her name. She felt as if that would be betraying her father. "Papa could not help himself; he said so. 'lndeed he could not, because he loves me and would not do it if he could."

"But he must be suffering to know about you. Tell me how we may find him. It cannot be right to keep him in ignorance." "If you did I should go away at once," said Ruby, firmly. "But is there no one you could trust?" "Yes, my cousin, who taught me that it was wrong. He is kind and good. "Where does he live?" Ruby shook her head. "I don't know. I told you I lost the address of the only person who knows him. Oh, you must not tell anyone. I will earn my living. I will do anything but go back to that man!" "I will ask my son, dear. We must do what is right." That evening Ruby came downstairs for a little while, and lay on the sofa in the pretty drawing- room. A refined life with ladies was all so new to her. _ _ _

After dinner Dr. Murray, the middle-aged man who had brought Ruby to his mother, came in to see her. Ruby looked so pretty that it was not difficult for anyone to be kind to her. She blushed as she held out her hand. She remembered now that she had been rude. to him.

"How kind you have all been to me," she said. "Thank you very, very much." Dr. Murray's face expressed a quiet pleasure which Ruby saw, but could not understand. "I am sorry you have been ill. You were quite overdone that day you lost your way." "I was feeling so miserable and I had so little money left that perhaps Mrs. Scott would have turned me out. Dr. Murray, did you ask about my bag ? " "Yes, but Mrs. Scott would not give it up. She said that you would call yourself for it. I told her you were with friends, but-" "I must go and get it some day it you will shew me the way." Dr. Murray looked at his sister. "You can do without it, dear," she said. - Mrs. Murray came into the room. "Arthur," she said, "our little friend, Ruby, says that we must not write to her father. What do you say ? " "Miss Ruby must judge for herself. There is no need to hurry. But her father ought to know that she is safe. "Not" said Ruby, decidedly. "Papa was going to let me go to Australia. I should have been lost to him, so I do not see why I should let him know." _ _

"When you are quite well you must think it over. If you like I will call and see any friend you may name." "I have only one friend—my; Cousin Alec; but Ido not know where he is now." Ruby felt now how much she had been kept a prisoner all her life.

"I lived in Australia when I was quite little with my mother, but papa lived away then. He never liked living in one place. When mamma died he came to fetch me and brought me to England, and we have lived in the country ever since."

When Ruby went upstairs to bed the three Murrays were left alone to discuss the strange event of Ruby's arrival among them. Dr. Murray was a retired doctor. He, had wandered about in his youth, but an uncle having left him a large fortune, .he had retired from prac- tice to devote himself to scientific research. "That she is good and quite innocent of the ways of the world there is no doubt," said Mrs. Murray, looking at her son. "0 Arthur, how thankful we ought to be that you found her at that moment. I shudder to think what would have happened if she had fallen into that wicked woman's clutches." "But there is some mystery about her if she will not tell us her father's name," put in the dauzliter.

"I think it is exactly as she says—that her' father wanted her to marry a man she could not love or respect, and that she fled from home," said Dr. Murray. "I knew that you would be good to her, mother. For the rest she has been vat" much frightened, and we must not hurry her, think we ought to promise to keep her secret.° "But we may get very much blamed when lies father finds her."

"She must go back of her own free will." "You are always so• trusting, Arthur," said his sister Jessie. "I could not doubt such a face."

"But we must do something with her," said Miss Murray, "as we are all going away in a fortnight." "Poor child! She is so grateful and patient, but she has a strong character," said Mrs. Murray. "She seems to have been purposely kept.back- wards. Everything seems strange to her." "If we only knew her name," said Jessie Murray. The next time that Ruby saw Dr. Murray was a few days after the last conversation, when she was allowed to walk downstairs. Mrs. Murray and her daughter had to go out together and left Ruby alone in the drawing-room with a book. Dr. Murray lo eked in by chance and came up to speak to her. "Oh, I wanted to see you," said Ruby, who seemed less shy with men than with women.

"What can I do for you ? " "I want to tell you that I am well now, and I want to go and earn my living." "Indeed, you are not quite strong enough yet." '"Yes, I feel strong, and I cannot repay yslu •as I have no money; but I could work." "I will ask ray mOther about it. But you know, Miss Ruby, it is difficult to find work for a young lady without a surname." Ruby smiled. "I had not thought of that." "Would you like to adopt another? It is not illegal, I believe," he laughed. "How difficult everything is," said Ruby, her eyes filling with tears. "Let me help you." "May I tell you, and will you promise me not to tell? I will not go home till lam sure that that horrid man has gone, and that I shall not be made to marry him. I will not." Dr. Murray looked a little amused as he said: "If you will trust me, I promise not to reveal anything without your leave." "My father is called Sir Harry Beauworth. We live at Yewdale, and the man I was to marry is- called James Felton; but I won't marry him. All the Baauworths were very decided people." Rub? laughed through her tears. "And c)u don't want to change your name? It is a name I am familiar with. I knew a Harry Beauworth in Australia." "Then it must have been papal He only became Sir Harry when we came to England." "My friend was a very original man, but Ido not think he would have wished to marry his daughter agaLast her will. As far as I knew he was not married." "How odd; but it may have been another. Is it a common name ? " "I did not think so, but one never knows how a name may crop up. Besides, I lost sight of my friend. He went into the interior. He had a passion for exploring, and I heard that he had died, or people thought that he had died in the waterless region. But your father may have married on his return, and he may be my old* friend. I had quite lost sight of him, and he, too, would not have thought that I shoulkreturn to England." "Why not ? " "Because I was determined never to see England again; but when my father died I thought bettor- of it and came back to live with my mother, who is a saint. . I could not refuse her anything." "Do you think that I could take the name of Smith?" said Ruby, suddenly. "Have you no second name ? " "Oh, yes, Ruby Halkett Beauworth. My mother's name was Halkett." "May we call you Halkett ? " "Yes, Ido not mind that. But you will keep my secret ? " "Certainly. And now I think of it perhaps my mother will be able to find you some suitable work. What can you do best ? " "I can't teach anything, for I have been taugh so little myself; but I like reading aloud, for I often read to Nancy." "That will do very well. You shall read to an old lady, a friend of ours who wants a companion. She will be very kind, only it will be a little'dtill as she never sees anyone." "I shall like that best. Oh, if you knew how frightened I was!" The frightened look returned to the girl's eyes, and Dr. Murray turned the conversation. (To be continued.)

AN EXTRAORDINARY NOVEL.

A p..werful novel, anzi one of cistrar,:dinary characmtics Yu more w.ny. than one, is L. T. Meade's " All Sorts." Dr il,ep human isitere2t—for it is one of those hooks wlsicis one cannet put down until it is finished its sit-crre moral tone, and the little-known sides of life whose sectets it reveals wits unflagging interest to the end, endear it to all fiction-lovers. The charming and Unc,,uventinnal heroine, daughter of an aristocratic family-, is introduced to the reader in the midst of fashionable life, portrayed with a sure band. Duchesses and countesses are her friends. In a moment, by a transition truly dramatic, the girl stands alone, her friends aloft, her fortune gone, her helpless laciy-rricther ruined in purse and spirit. The ad- ventures of the girl and the story of the two who wooed and the One whe wedded her, form a drama we will not spoil by anticipating the story. It is a book, tor, of remarkable courage. In a passage which has led to great discussion in literary circles and caused the authoress to be interviewed by a London newspaper as to its motive, 'he has lilt hesitated to introduce the name of a widely advertised article. When questioneri on this point, the authoress disclaimed all knowledge of the gossip it had (as all the world knows) created. She was, in fact, asked bluntly whether this was a paid advertisr meat, and as bluntly replied that it was cm,- tainlr not FO.

" I have," she said, "a motive in every thing I write —to perrray accurately the circumstances which I try to describe. Here I was writing a conversation. I asked myself what is the sort of thing a woman would be likely to say when her friend remarked that she had been suffering from indigeetion, I considered that bee impulse cenuld be to recommend something. I have heard these pills highly spoken of for indigestion : I have beard doctors speak favourably of them, andjaf course, I have read in the eewspapers what people say they have done for them. Consequently, this was the firet remedy that occurred to me, and I named it. I - wanted to make the conversation life like. I wanted (as I always do) to do good work, and I should have written any thing—anything," Mrs. Meade repeated, " which eeemt d to me to conduce to that end." The passage referred to is as follows " I have, suffered a good deal lately from indigestion,' replied mother in her gent'e tones. " And a bad thing it is—a very bed thing," said Mrs. Ittnaing, "I cured myself with Dr. Williams' pink pills ' for reile people. Did you over try 'em, Mrs. Wickham? " No," replied mother, gravely. "W.ll, well ; they pulled me round. Albert was terrney cencerned about me a year ago. I couldn't tarct the gieetest dainties you could give me. I turned against my 100., and as to going upstairs, why if you'll believe me, I ceuld have no more teken possession of that attic next to your young daughter than I could have fled. Nev there isn't a stair in Btitain would daunt me ; I'd te good for climbing the Monument any flue morn- ing, and it's all owing to Williams' pink pills. They're are a grand medicine." • sOf course a reference of this sort is almost unex empled in literary hittlry ; but there is no question of- the author's good faith ; and, indeed ; this subject much "in the air "just now, and the attention excited be the reports that have been publi,bed by the proprie- ter. of Dr. Williams' pink pills concerning the cures effected account for the interest which they attract. Tb re is hardly a village, and certainly not a town, in this country, that does not. possess its local " miracle " some invalid or cripple, or some Pickle man or woman, made ttrong and well by Dr. Williams' pink pills. They are even call to have made ther very into royal circles; and the Court Journal, more than a year ago, commented on their popularity in the littlest ranks of Society. " The practice of pink ' pill-taking, so lavishly en- couraged by bold edvertisernent," slid roy city's fa- voured chronicle, "is said to be making upward pro- gress in Seciety, and report has it that the product of the I. only genuine ' manufacturer—the pill with the coven-warded name so often displayed before our eyes with piteous entreaty to shun all pills *with a ' missing word' in their title—has penetrated to the most exalted tildes. -- The monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council was held in this Council Chamber at the Town Hall 011 Tuesday evening. The Mayor presided and there were also present :—Alderman Wright (deputy Mevor),Alder- man Sutton, Aleermen Huichinson, Bolton,Firchugh, Francomb, Councillors Shaw Green, Greening, Monks, James Evans, Lee, Baird, Plumpton, Smeihuret. John Evans, Beaten, Bennett, F R Roberts. Rogers, A Jolley, Peaeock, Ellison, Duncan, ThltliOti, A. J Jolley,Cannell, L Greening, with J Lyon Whittle (town clerk.), Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk),Dr Gornall (medical officer), J Dees (sanitary engineer), r Longdin (borough sur- veyor), J Sutcliffe (assistant surveyor). W S Haddock (gas engineer). Watch Committee. The Pay for Police Reservists' Families. It was r?solved that the resignation of Sergeant Chewier be accepted, and that. be receive a pension for life equal to two thirds of his annual pay.—lt was further resolved: That the tender of Messrs McDowell's, Limited, to supply a horse ambulance at £77 10e be accepted. Mr C A Hindle having informed the commit- tee that the contract for the construction of the cells tied coexidors of the ne'ir police premises provided only for common bricks to be lime washed above the glaz d brick dado, and stated .hat the additional cost of sab- stituting white glazed bricks would be anout £3OO over contrect price, it was decided that white &zee bricks be substituted, that the Berough Surveyor ascertain the actual cost, and that public notihe be given of the new bylaws made under the Municipal Corporatkins Act, and which are now in force. At a meeting heldeon the 28th December, the following members of the force being Army Reservists, having been recalled to the colours :—P.C'e. Gibbs, Heso, Muskett, Bradley,and Kane, it was resolved that they be allowed leave of absence, without pay. That the following allowances be uncle to the wives and children or other dependents of the Reservists, viz.:—The weekly income of the family of a Reservist from all sources (except allotment from husband's pay and the proceeds of the wife's labour) to be made up to 10e., for each child not in work. 23 63. ; for other dependents 7s. That the following recom- mendations of the Chief Constable be adopted order to eope with the serious withdrawal from the effective strength. I beg to recommend that the monthly leave list be temporarily suspended, and that the t ao junior members of the Detective Department (Roper and Delaha.y) revert for the time being to uniform duty. I should also discontinue the attendance of a man at the Town Hall for the time being. As a recompense to all ranks for the deprivation of their day's leave, I would suggest that a day's extra pay per month be granted them in lieu thereof. The MAYOR, in moving the confirmation of the min- utes, referred to two or three of the resolutions. In re- gard to the proposed purchase of a horse ambulance the Council would be agreed that the time had come when Warrington should be possessed of such a conveyance, and the Watch Committee were well advised in ordering it. He was glad to say, also, although the competitien was keen, a Warrington firm had shown itself compe- tent to supply the ambulance as well as any outsider. With regard to the resolution for the substitution of white glazed bricke for lime-waleed common bricks it was found desirable to make the alterations for sanitary and other reasons. With reference to the proposed al- lowance to be made to the fathilies of Reservists who were members ef the police force they would have read the letter from the Home Office making ceetain recom- mendetions, but if they had followed them it would have upset altogether the arrangements already in force in connection with their own local relief committee. It was considered advisable to put everyone upon the same footing. Hence the resolution. Alderman SUTTON seconded. Councillor BaNearrr said he saw that the amount recommend in the communication from the Home Office was 2s more than their own. They ware all agreed that the dependents of these brave fellows aliould be treated in the most liberal manner prasible. Was there no possible way of increasing the amount? The MAYOR said the amount was considered to be sufficient. They had, of course, many other cases, and they had had no complaints. On the contrary.they had reeeleed many expressions of gratitude from the wives of those who had gone away. They did not wise to dis- turb the arrangements which had already been in force for some weeks. The difference Was not so great in the total, for it would be noticed that in the other recom- mendations the amounts for bee children were less, and unfortunately in many of their cases there were two, three, and even four children. Councillor MORNS pointed out that living was not so dear in Warrington as in London, and 103 here would go as far as 123 61 there. The minutes were confirmed. The Park Improvements. Widening of Kendrick-street. Opposition by Councillor Smethurst. The Street Improvement and Lighting Commileee has resolved that the General Purpeees Committee be recom- mended to purchase from Messrs. Faircloueh and Sons the piece of land in BOWley containing 15,500 square yards or thereabouts; at the price of lid. per square yard at 25 years' purchase; that the plan showing the line of frontage on the north side of Wash-lane be signed and deposited ; that Alderman Blackhutst and Councillor Heaton be appointed members of the. Special Property Purchaeing Committee ; and that Kenerink-street, co- extensive with the Park, be widened to 40 feet; and that the Town Hall. Park, and Baths Committee be requested to allow the improvement to be made. Alderman SUTTON moved the confirmetior of the minutes, and the motion was seconded by Councillor BAIRD. Coueciller SMETHURST Wild he protested against what he considered to be an extraordinary extravagance on the part of the committee so far as the widening of Kendrick-street was concerned. It was not his intention on that occasion to propose an amendment to the minutes, but the footpath on each side of the street was sft 6in wide. The width of the street was 20, t between the curbstones, and yet they were going to take 9ft off the Park to make it 4011 wide, and all this for a street in which 20 vehicles did not pass up in the course of a day on the way Vs Heath-side where they would have to go through a dangerous passage only 10ft wide, and where there had been several accidents. Be could not see why they wanted to widen a street so vary little used, and be considered the proposed expenditure an extravagance. Councillor BENNETT said that Councillor Smethurst had brought forward an eloquent argument for the widen- ing of Heath-lane, but he had advanced no argument against the widening of Kendrick-street. Hecontended that a street which abutted on the Park ought to be the same width as an ordinary residential street. He hoped they would widen the street, and that the amendment would not be pressed. The MAYOR: There is no amendment. C BENNETT : And then we will do our best to get Heath-lane widened. Alderman FRANCOMB said he had a great deal of sympathy with Councillor Smethurst, because Kendrick- street was very little used. There ought to be a good entrance at the corner proposed, and the improvement in Kendrick-street could wait until there was a more pressing need for it to be done. He should like to know when they would see something being done in Wilders- pool-road. (Hear, bear.) Alderman BOLTON remarked thet the footpath wss in a bad condition and should be taken up, and it was con- cluded that that Was the time also to widen the street. The wall was 'utiles and in a bad state of repair and tin CIEs and other things were thrown over that into the Park. Be thought the alterations proposed would effect a ereat improvement at little extra coat. Councillor Motacs said the resolution would have been more acceptable it it had been coupled with an estimate of the probable cost. Alderman Stenos; replied that he was not in a position to give that information, but they were told as a com- mittee that the well was in a dangerous condition. If the wall had to come down at a it was thought that it should be taken down at once. They had a good oppor- tunity of widening Kendrick street at little expense. The Borough Surveyor would prepare an estimat3 and this would he laid before them before the work was pro- ceeded with. Councillor SMETHURST hoped that after what had been said be several of the members that the Council would take the hint as a friendly one,and-- The MAYOR: You may make a personal explanation but not a speech. -

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY JANUARY 12, 1900

Councillor SBIRTRURST : Then I beg to propose as an arhendment that- The M&YOR : You cannot now; too late. The minutes were then agreed to. The Hospital Extensions. Lively Discussion. The Health COMMiLtee in addition to acceptine ten- ders for the erection of a ward pavilion and administra- tive block resolved that the Town Clerk be instructed to apply to the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing of the sum of £5,500 for the purpose of hospital extension, and that the temporary wood pavilion on the land adjoining the hospital be taken down and the materials stored for use in case of further emergency. Alderman SUTTON moved, and the MAYOR seconded, the clnfirmatian of th.e minutes. Councillor PLUMPTON opposed the paesingof the minutrs with refer.oce to the hospital question. Be hoped that evertjavernber of the Council would seriously consider the action which had been taken by the Health Committee i in deciding to retain the Infectious Diseases Hospital in its present position. In his opinion toe question was a most serious one—the most serious which nal been con- I sidered since he had been a member of the Council. An erroneous statement had been unintentionally made as to the cost of the present building and land. It had been slatted that the premises had cost £16,000, whereas as a matter of fact they had not cost half that amount according to the figures which had bcen presented by the Teem Clerk. 11 the Beard of Guardians had been, asked a reasonable amount— Alderman Suwon : Don't you think this is a little bie out of order ? Whatever was dons by the Board cannot les helped now. We made an offer and they re-' fused it ; and it is only a waste of time to go Leto that question. , Councillor PLUMPTON : No doubt you think so. The MA-eon: Any remark as to the proposal of the Guardians is out of order, but Councillor Plumpton is, right in discussing the situation of the hospital. Couneillor YLUItIPTON, continuing, contended that the erection of a new hospital would not be the loss to the ratepayers, they might think it would be owing to the falsnishaeement which had been inscle.lf the hospital had only cost from £6,000 to £7,000 instead of £16,000 it would be no great loss to the town to do away with it altogether, and erect one outside the borough. They had purchased three lots of land for the feeer hospital, and for the price paid for them and the price of the isuildings they could get every penny back by selling tile land where the present building was. Land had been offered near Sankey Chapel House, and they could iset it for £1,400 or £1,500. The patients would have the benefit of pure air during convalescence,which thy had not now. Buildings woco being pot up all arouni the pre sent hospital, and very shortly it would be surrounded by works and' chimneys that must be projudical to the patients. He therefore, in these Circumstances, moved as an arne.ndlnent that the minutes be referred back .to the COannit:eo as far as the estimates were cerscerned, and that the desirability of erecting a building outside the town be considereL They had borrowed £7,000 and they were going to spsnd £5,000 more. In addition to that there would eventually ha a lodge erected at further expense. The lodge was not mentioned there, but he believed it was intended to erect it eventually. The MAYOR: I must ask you net to make any further remarks about that. Councillor Peurorross said he •=oved his amendment beeanse lie considered that the disadventagee of having the hespital in its present situation, both to inmates and residents in the neighbourhood were to be endured to suir the coavenience of the Medical Officer of Health. Some £12,000 or £15,000 was going to be spent and they would then only be able to accommodate the number of patients that bed been in the building the past year. All he desired was to see the Infectious Diseases Hospital outside the Borough of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Councillor BENNETT said he sympatbised so strongly with the views which had been given expression to by C o.iior PlumptOo that be should second the amend- ment. It was contrary to all the recognised principles of sanitation to erect a fever hospited in the centre of a district which was fairly populated now, and was likely to be densely populated in the near future. He must confess that he was pleased with the plans, and he thought the buildings to be erected would do credit to the town, but when they were contenc.plating the exa pendieure cf from £12,000 to £15,000 they ought to re- consider the whole matter, and decide once for all whether it WaS wise to have a fever hospital in the midst of a thickly populated district. The mistake made with the Board of Guardians was irreparable. The MAYOR ! There was no mistake. Councillor BENNETT : At any rate it is no use crying over spilled milk. The MAYOR : No milk has been spilled in this case. Councillor BENNETT : I think it is a mistake to spend money towards perpetuating a mistake which bas been made. - Councillor JAMES Evens supported the amendment,l and said there was a strong feeling in the ward against the proposed extension. Alderman SUTTON: We know there is. Councillor James EVANS : A great many residents have expressed their disapproval of the scheme. A very in- fluential deputation waited on the Health Committee on this very question. The MAYOR I think the deputation expressed them- selves perfectly satisfied with the proposed extension if the lodge was not erected. (Hear, hear.) They had no objection to the extension. Alderman SUTTON: That was so. THE LATE MR. D. W. FINNEY.

The Funeral.

The grave closed over the remains of the deceased gentleman at noon last Thursday in St. Paul's Churchyard. A short service was conducted in St, Paul's Church by the Rev. E. It. Tarbuck (vicar). Several hymns were sung, the singing be- ing led by the choir under the conductorship of Mr. Wilkes. the organist and choirmaster. The ser- vice at the graveside was, wisely, brief and was conducted by the Rev. H. V. Figot, vicar of St. Thomas's and formerly vicar of St. Paul's. OLD • FOLK'S TREAT.

Dinner and Tea at the Parr Hall.

The old men's dinner and aged womea's tea is an old inetitution which few would care to see aban- doned. To say that it is appreciated by the aged poor of the town, would be putting it mildly, for by some it is looked upon as a " lied Letter "day in their lives. The committee which has the ar- rangements in hand consists of a number of well known local& gentlemen, Alderman Sutton being the president,eir P Chrimes,the treasurer,end Alderman J E Wright and Mr T A Sutton, the hon secretaries. This year's treat was held last Thursday in the Parr Hall and it is perhaps a sign that there is less dis- tress in the town, that the number of those who at- tended was a considerable decrease on previous years. At n00n,260 old nests their ages rengiug from 60 to 85 at down ti an eaceilent rapast, and needless to say ample justice was done to the abundance of good things pro- vided. The fare consisted of roast joints, the mat be- ing of the hest, potatoes and other vegetables, plum pudding, and bread and cheese, and each man was allowed a quantity of beer. After dinner pipes and packets of tobacco were handed round. The catering was in the bands of Mr and Mrs R Shackleton and gave the greatest satisfaction, as was shown by the smiling faces of those who partook of the spread. Amongst these who attended during the morning were the Mayor (Alderman Henry Roberts), the Rector (Rev Canon Willis), the Revs E C E Carleton. A S Cameron and Fathers Folding and Whittle, Alderman and Mrs J E Wright, Alderman Sutton, Coun- cillors Burton, Alfrei Jolley, Smethurst, and Dr Connell Mr and Mrs D Fanshaw, Mr T Longdin, Mr George Pilliee, Mr H Tof t. Mr C Madely, Captain Waring. Mr Firers. Thornton, Mr John Sampson, Mr M Knowles,Miss Gibbons, Miss Gimes Burchall, Mrs Smethurst, Mrs Pilling, Mrs Banks and many others. Mr W S Woods played a number of selections on the organ. At the nu- clei eon of the meal Alderman SUTTON in the course of a few remarks said he hoped they bad 411 heartily enjoyed their dinner and in the name of the committee he had much pleasure in wishing them a very happy and prosperous New Year. (Hear, hear.) They had new arrived at the year 1900, and although they might not have reached the twentieth century he hoped that they should all meet again next year when at all events, they would have lived until that eventful period. When they met in that hall last year he ventured to express the belief that the New Year would be a prosperous one, that there would be lgood work and good wages for ever, becly and be was very incased to think that his wish had ben gratified, for he believed that the past year had been a prosper- ous and successful one for eeeryone of them. Notwithstanding that the country was now eugateed in the unfortunate war which had broken out in South Africa, he still sincerely honed that the same prosperity would continue and that they would enjey the the same plentiful harvest during toe present year ae they had done in the past. He must remind them that on the occasion of those gatherings they never missed drinking the health of Her Majesty the Queen and never, in his opinion was there an occasion during the long period of Her Majesty's reign when their sympathies were more necessary or desirable than at the present time. What Her Majesty's anxiety as to the success of her soldiers and the honour of Great Britain must to. could be better imagined than described. He hoped the war would aeon be brought to a happy close without much bloodshed,and that peace and prosperity wouldreie,nover the land not only during the remainder of Her Majesty's reign, but for a very leng period to come. Referring to the state of the town he said that if they required any evidence to prove to them that there was not so much want among the working people this year as there had been before, he thought it was quite evident from the fact that they had not the number present that day within nearly ose hundred, as they bad on the previous year. He must say there was a great improvement at all events in their appearance, and it showed that they had not suff .red so many hardships during the past year as they had endured during former years. (Applause.) The toast was heartily drunk, and the company sang the National Anthem, Mr. D. Fanshaw taking the solo. Alderman WRIGHT said he was very pleased to be amongst them and wished them with all heartinecs a happy New Year. (Applaute.) The RECTOR after expressing his pleasure at being afforded the opportunity of speaking on that occasion, said he wanted them, to think of three things for a moment. The first was their national interest. There never was a time when they were more interested than at present in the affairs of the nation. Most of them were old men, and perhaps they coati recollect the Crimean War and the Indian Mutiny. Since then there had never been a time of greater anxiety to the Govern- ment and to all of them. Ile thought they had every hope that by God's blessing lasting peace and good Government would be secured. They were all immensely interested in the affairs of the town. Menicheelinterest at the present time must enema() and interest them all. They had seen wonderful improvements in Warrington during the last ten years, or even since he came to the town. If the programme which had been announced was carried out they should have wonderful chances in the next ten years,which he hoped they would all live to see. They were told that they were to have electric light, electric trams, a new water works, new bridges and wider streets, and perhaps many other improvements. He only hoped that the cost of all these things would not feel upon them but utters younger men better able to bear the burden. The Rector then referred to their personal individual and re- lieious interests. He thought he must congratulate them all upon their moderation that day. Sobriety was one of the great virtues they had to try and encourage and he hoped their examples might be for the good of the younger generation. In conclusion he hoped that in the next ten years, or in much shorter time there might be a better observance of God's Day. Ile wished them a very happy New Year—(applatne)—aed propossd avote of thanks to the committee and those who had prepared the dinnsr. One of the old men seconded the motion and several expressed their gratitude to the committee and to the ladies, One of them declared that" the dinner was good enough for the Queen to s:t down to." Such a dinner he never had in his life. Alderman SUTTON responded on behalf of the coat- naittee. Mr. P. CHRIMES also addressed the gathering and wished the old men a happy and prosperous New Year, (Applause.) Mr. GEORGE &errs then gave an effictive recital of the "Absent.minded Beggar" and the sineiug of "The Soldiers of the Queen" by M. Faoshew concluded the proceedings. At 5.30 in the afternoon about three hundred women aged from 60 to 94, were regaled with a substantial tea, and afterwards the usual entertainment was held. Alderman Sutton presided and the Mayor and Mayoress were also present There was a. large attendance of the public, and an interesting and varied programme was submitted. The proceedings commenced 'with the hearty singing of the National Anthem by all present, Mr D Fanshaw taking the sole. Songs by Mr George Clay, Mr Beale, ad Mr L Aukland, dances by Miss Arline Harrison aed party, and ventriloquial 1 sketches by Mr A Grounds followed and were all well re ceived.tdi A Grounds was particularly successful with his contributions. Mr W S Woods presided at the organ and Mr W H Payton ably accompanied. During the interval, Mr George Smith rscited Hipline's " Absent- minded Beggar" and a collection was made. The Chairman and Councillor Smethurst having addressed the meeting ThetMeeoa in the count) of a short speech, said he was very pleased to be present that evening as he had never had the pleasure of spending much time at those gatherings. Personally he was very much obliged to the ladies and gentlemen who engaged themselves in preparing for those dinners and teas. They were all very fond of old Warrington, especially those who had been born in the town, and, at the same time those peeple who had not been born in Warrington, but who had come to reside there, had got quite inoculated with a love for the place. He was gladito see the number of old people present was not so numerous as formerly as it showed that the prosperity of the town had been greater during the past year than it had been for some time. His old friend would say that was Irceuse they had a Tory Government in offies. but it was clear all people did not agree on that point. They bad. however, an improved condition of things. At the present time they were under a cloud because of the sad war in Soath Africa. Pr end as he was of Wartington, he never was so proud as he was now, to see how splendidly the townspeop e had re- sponded with regard to the subscriptions to the war fund. They had shown a determination that the wives and children of the men who had gone out so bravely to do the work of the Empire should net suffer during their hus- bands and fathers absence. He hoped that Institutien which had been carried en in Warrineton so successfully for fifty years, might co venue for fifty times as many years, and that the old folks whose circumstanees in life might not have been so prosperous as their fellow citizens, would not be forgotten at Christmas time, when all tried to be mere or less happy and bright. (Ap- plause).

WARRINGTON ANGLERS'

ASSOCIATION,

Annual Meeting. The annual meeting of the above association was held on Tnursday last weekot the Druids Hall,Sankey-street, and was well attended. The chair was occopied during the earlier part of the evening by Ifr. L. Greening, and later by Mr. J. Lyon Whittle. Amongst others present were Cvunciilor James Evans, Mes:irs G. S. Srneeth, secretary of the Woreley Canal Boare,T Driver,secretary of the County Palatine Anglers' Association, Manchester J. Mawson, E. Gilb..trt, H. Kent, J. M. Ma,yhall, W. A.

Proud, etc. The annual report was submitted and showed the association to be in a very flourishing condition. It stated that the total number of members, including 22 members of the juvenile branch was 386. As illustrating the popularity of the aseo- elation member bad jsined it from 32 town- ships of Lancashire and Cheshire. With reference to their oldest private water, that at Mickle Trafford, the committee regretted that they had to repeat their remarks of last year, "that the fishing had been an absolute failure." The weeds had in places almost blocked up the watercourse, thus rendering successful angling almost out of the question. The Lymm water had not fished so well during the year- The fish there did not come on the feed till late le the season and the committee by way of improving the fishing had determined to introduce new stock. In the last annual report, mention was made of negotiations being then carried on for the right of fish- ing in another water. The committee had now to say that their efforts had been succes3ful, and they had secured the length of the riser Rollin, from Bollington Mill to Warburton Mill, on bath sues of the river, and from Warburton Mill to the Ship Canal on the Warburtun side. These rights had been obtained on most advantageous terms. When the transfer had been completed they at once sot about stocking the water, and obtained from the Wyresdale Fishery Co., 1,000 yearling Rainbow trout, which arrived in the pink of condition, au i they were placed in the river without the loss of a single fish. Quite recently, through the muni- ficence of their est_pmed Cflairmau, Mr J Lyon Whittle, they had feceived two consignments of splendid trout. comprising Rainbow, Loch Lovuns, I and Farb:), ranging up co a pound in weight, to the number of 700. 'these nere duly transferred to the River Rollin with but the les of three fish. Arrange- ments were also being m a for the puichase of 20,000 Patio o va,which the Cas .rinan (Mr. Whittle) had kindly consented to hatch our fer them ; 1,000 of these, he prc. sented to the associatiee. It was only due to the Cnair- man that they should seeress their appreciation of the splendid gift be had leads to the society. Their best thanks were also due to their esteemed President Mr. E. M. Crosfield who so successfully conducted the negotiations tor the requisition of that water. With re- ference to the Old Quay Canal, ill luck had again de- prived them of a very valuable .fishing ground. ORiug to pumping operations at Walton Arches an unusual quantity of bad water rushed into the canal and destroyed a considerable number of fish: The River Weaver, generally had not been productive of very good sport during ths past season ; in fact,all local waters had yielded but indifferent sport: The Moulton Parish Council had approached the London and North- Western Railway Co., with a view to a station being erected at Newbridge, but so far, they had not receivlct a definite reply. Should a petition ;,o the Company th found necessary it was to be hoped that all Lancashire anglers would support it, as a station at that point would give easy access to a long reach of excellent fishing ground. Too committee had been occupied with a proj!ct to enclose the fish in the Lymm water and also to prevent the inrush of the bad water from the Ship Canal. A general outline of the work was prepared and submitted to experts for au estimate of the cost. This was placed at such a large figure as to render the project • abselutely beyond our means and for the present the idea was abandoned. The angling contests of the past year had been fairly successful, the attendances being some what better than in previous years and consequently more valuable prizes were competed for. In conclusion the committee appealed with confidence for support,. with the assurance that they had only the best interests of the members at heart and an earnest iledre to place the Associetion on a firm footing and raise it to the highest standard of efficiency. (Ape' slue.) ELECTION OF ()EMUS.
