MR. BALFOUR has always Cheerful Fatalism, been regarded as a philo- sopher, but of late he has posed as a cheerful fatalist. His replies to criticisms, by no means wholly from one political side, have only served to provoke more antagonism, and that from his own side. He regards it as quite unnecessary to offer any apology whatever for the Government, and he has proceeded to develop an elaborate fatalistic argument. Mr. OHAINIRIMLAIN is the author of the phrase that war was "inevitable," and Mr, BALFOUR has tacked on to his argument two other inevitables. It was inevitable, he thinks, that we should be caught unprepared, and that blunders and disasters should occur to British troops in the field he also regards as inevitable. Surely never before was a Cabinet Minister driven to such des perate straits. Our diplomatic skill, our Parlia- mentary system, our military organisation, are all responsible only in so far as there is some fatal- ism at work. The Boers have organised an artillery, obtained European adventurers by thousands, secured successes in the field baffled an 1 beaten our generals, and Mr. BALFOUR looks on with cheerful fatalism at our ignorance, supineness, want of preparation, and incapacity to achieve any striking success. He does not believe that anybody can bo blamed. If our artillery was defective and not sufficiently abundant, if we were ignorant of the success of French gunrnakers in devising a big mobile gnu outranging anything in Europe, if we were caught napping, if we had done nothing to make invasion difficult, and if we selected a general who had to be superseded with- in two months of his arrival at the theatre of war, it was all part of the inevitable which sits so easily upon the strongest Ministry of modern times. It will be very difficult for patriotic Englishmen and for Englishmen who are not party politicians to have patience with such stuff and nonsense. The present Government had been duly warned of our artillery deficiencies, and it ought to have been fully informed of what the Transvaal was doing. It had been talking about a policy of" thorough" at the time its final attitude over the negotiations was assumed, and it deceived Parliament and the country as to the sufficiency of our military and financial preparations. Disasters in the field have proved, however, the defects of our prepara- tions, equipments, and strategy. Mr. BALFOUR calls these "inevitable incidents.' If we could accept this fatalistic explanation, we should be compelled by stress of logic to accept our final defeat in South Africa as equally inevitable, and all the spasmodic efforts now being made as de- monstrations of human folly. It is not in this spirit the coantry is rousing itself to action at the present moment. It believes that men are responsible for events, and that Ministers cannot shelter themselves behind any theory about the "inevitable." When the' time comes it will ex- press its opinions upon the conduct of the Colonial Office, the War Office, and the Treasury in no un-

certain tones,and if the Liberals had been stronger; more united, and more resolute, Parliament would not be in session'a month before it would hurl from office an incapable Government and its fatalistic apologists. It is the fate which has been deserved, and will only be postponed.

AN excellent demonstration University Representation. in favour of restoring University representation to its original idea has been made by Sir EDWARD FRY and Sir J. G. FITCH. The electors of London University have to elect a successor to Sir JOHN LUBBOCK, and the Liberal Unionists ,and the Liberals have selected candidates. Men of dis- tinction and personal influence, men eminent in literatute and science, men who are not notably party politicians,ought to find their way to Parlia- ment by the Universities. This is the argument, and its plausible character is obvious. Only on these grounds can we defend the continuance of University representation and the endowment of graduates with a vote over and above that which they have as residents in boroughs and counties. The contention is sound,but it comes too late. It did not hold good in Sir JonN LUBBOCK'S case, and only one or two cases have occurred in which it has been evident that something more than party politicians were enabled to enter the House of Commons. We have had vehement partisans, nearly all of one political colour, constantly returned by University electors. Men of light and leading have been as few and far between as angels' visits. We can run over the present repre- sentatives of the Universities. They all sit on one side of the House. They are all partisans, and some of them are not even men of eminence in science, literature, and the arts. The fact is that University representation has become an anomaly, and the sooner it comes to an end the better. It no longer serves any useful purpose, and the farce cannot be maintained that it enables eminent men to be returned who could not get into Parliament in any other way. The proof of this is to be found in the voting. Whenever a strictly party question comes up the University M.P.'s are always found supporting the Conservative side in politics. On education and scientific questions they do the same. The Whips of the party would rub their eyes and be astonished if some night all the men of "light and leading" in the present House of Commons, except Sir John Gorst, who is a member of the Adminis- tration, were to vote against the Government on some educational matter. This shews to what a degraded condition University tepresentation has come. We have endowed University graduates with a second vote ibecause it is assumed they are wiser than the ordinary electors. But when they elect M.P.'s who are as much partisans as the M.P.'s elected by the men in the street, where is the difference? In what way are the interests of science and culture better taken care of? There is hardly ever a contest for a University seat with- out some awkward question being raised. It is so now. Dr. Comarrs, the Liberal candidate, is so much of a London politician that he is objected to on that account. Is not Mr. CARSON a party politician? Have the M.P.'s for Cambridge and Oxford given half a dozen non-party votes since they were elected? The very arguments used against Dr. Comarts apply to Mr. BUSK, and they make our case so much the stronger. Happily, the time is near when University M.P.'s will cease to exist, because they are an anomaly and a dis- appointment.

Here and there powerful voices are making themselves heard in favour of merciful treatment for the revolted subjects of the Queen who, after being warned in the name of the Queen, rose up in rebellion, and were captured by Colonel Pilcher's column near Douglas. If by merciful treatment is meant a fair civil trial, nobody will be inclined to object. But if freedom from punish- ment after guilt has been made manifest is meant, we hope the Imperial authorities will be mindful of their obligations to the Empire at large, and refuse to entertain a plea so opposed to all hopes of good government. Is the loyal colonist who declines to rebel and has his farm devastated in revenge to be the only sufferer, while his neigh- bour who rebels not only keeps his goods intact but escapes without punishment also ? Anything so ridiculous would be flat incite- ment to rebellion. Moverover, we do not see why the customary penalty should net be inflicted upon captured rebels in its entirety. They have been blessed all their lives with good government under the Queen's sway; they have derived moral and material advantages from the Imperial connection which has helped to build up their prosperity. When in danger of being led away they were fairly warned, and they refused to obey the warning. They acted thus with their eyes wide open, and because of their own cupidity. They shot down I without remorse the soldiers of the Queen, whose subjects they were. A clearer case for severe punishment, supposing the disloyal acts are proved cannot well be imagined. If such punishment is not inflicted the crime of treason might as well be removed from the code.

The forecasts for the coming session do not ap- pear very hopeful that the lot of the aged poor will be lightened. We may regard old-age pen- sions as dead for the present. As an alternative we were offered an extension of outdoor relief, but even this crumb of comfort may be too much for the digestive powers of this Parliament. Mean- while starvation is claiming the aged poor. In a Yorkshire district an old labourer, incapable of the lightest toil, had half-a-crown weekly from the guardians. One-and-threepence had to go for rent ; the rest kept life in him for a few weeks,but was insufficient to keep his strength from ebbing steadily, until finally the lamp of life flickered and died out. The man died of starvation,hideously pro- longed by the State. A witness at the inquest said he knew several old people who were being slowly done to death in the samr) tedious fashion. Would it not be less hypocritical if the State threw these people into prison and refused thorn food alto- gether? Death would come more quickly, at any rate. At present we profess to support life when, in truth. we are slowly abstracting it, as these un- happy cases too clearly show. We do not suppose that this Yorkshire district is exceptional. There must be hundreds of cases of slow starvation owing to insufficient relief. They could go into the union of course ; but they do not; and this indisposition to sacrifice the last shred of self-respect remaining does not exonerate the nation.

The reality of the war is continually being brought borne to us. When soldiers are not ac- tually, visibly, and daiiy departing for the front ' there is something happening to keep our a tetir tion fixed on the deadly struggle in South AL iCa. If waris not unique, many of the incidents in con- nection with the present campaign are certainly unique in themselves. The Mayor and townspeople of Warrington share I on Sunday in a ceremonial unprecedented in the annals of the good old town, and connected directly with the war. This was nothing less than the depositing of the colours of the Third South Lancashire Militia in charge of the Mayor, during the time the battalion seeks glory and fair renown on the burning African veldt. Neither militia nor colours have yet " seen service," but this fact is of small account and entered not into the aspect of things on Sunday. The men were going—that was sufficient—and they are desirous of honouring Warrington, which has been so long; their headquarters with the custody of the regi- mental colours. His Worship, the Mayor, appro.

dated fully the solemnity of the occasion, and it was fittingly celebrated by devotion and ceremonial at the Parish Church and Town Hall. Warring- ton has now an equal interest in two battalions— the Ist South Lancashire, and the Militia—and we are sure it is the sincere hope of all the town- people that both will achieve the glory they covet and return in safety to home and fatherland.

Outside its barders,we wonder how many people in the district have any idea of the magnitude the Warrington Anglers' Association has attained. Though it has been in existence for nine years, it is not an Association one hears much about,but for some time now it has been doing a large and im- portant work. The membership is close upon 400 and is still going up, applications coming in from anglers in almost all parts of Lancashire and Cheshire. For 7s 6d a year a workingman may become possessed of the privilege of fishing valu- able preserved waters in both counties; he may travel to his destination at a reduced ;railway rate, and he may enjoy himself thoroughly and beneficially at very little cost. The society .does an excellent work in taking these toilers away from their more or less miserable surround- ings into the country and emancipating them from the baneful attractions of the beerhouse. Not' only does it accomplish much in this direction, but it does .not a little towards ensuring the non- pollution of streams which ought to be "things of beauty and a joy for ever" if manufacturersdid not look upon them as natural ' sewers. There is some influence behind the Warrington Association for such men as Mr. E. M. Crosfield, Councillor L. Greening, and the Town Clerk are numbered among its officers. To them much of its power, many of its privileges, and its reputation as the strongest society in Lancashire or Cheshire, the Warrington Angling Association owes.

The new County Court Judge for Warring ton was last Thursday •cordially welcomed and congra- tulated upon his appointment when he took his seat to preside over the proceedings for the first time. His Honour acknowledged the co mpli- ments in fitting terms, and after paying a tribute to the services of his predecessor, proceeded to emphasize his intention of completing the business at each sitting before the rising of the Court and preventing, if possible, the adjournment of any ot the cases down for hearing. In his desire to facilitate the business of the Court His Honour .will have many well-wishers. It is notorious that in the Courts of Justice, and in County Courts especially, a great deal of time is unnecessarily wasted' by clients, witnesses and others, owing to the slow progress made in getting through the cases to be heard. His Honour gave an earnest of his good intentions by quickly despatching the business of the day. By his sagacity,his courteous disposition and business-like methods he gained the good opinions of those present, and there is every reason to believe that the favourable impressions made will be lasting.

His Honour is a member of a distinguished legal family. He is a nephew of Lord James of Hereford, the Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancas- ter, and his father is a well-known solicitor of Hereford. His Honour is the Recorder of Here- ford, and " meMbers of the legal profession are wondering," says a Manchester contemporary, "whether he will:have to resign the recordership in view of his new appointment. The Lord Chan- cellor is said to have strong opinions about one man holding the posts of recorder and county court judge, but he appears to be rather chary about enfercing his views. He insisted iti one or two instances upon the new judge resigning the recordership, although he has allowed others to continue to act both as recorcter and judge of the county court. The humbler members of the pro- fession wo•ild be glad to see the Lord Chancellor have the ccurage of his convictions, for it would mean a large number of combrtable berths being thrown open."

THE FORTHCOMING ARTS AND

CRAFTS EXHIBITION.

The Arrangements. During the w=.ek, preparatory work has been done for the building of the annexe which is to adjoin Parr Hall, and which is being built specially for the Warrington and District Arts and Crafts Ex- hibition, to be opened on March 3rd. The exhibition is being promoted by a local committee of which Mr. Ralph Johnson is chairman,Mr. William Roberts treasurer, and Messrs C. E. King and W. H. Pigot, joint secretaries. The committee think that the closing year of the century should be marked by an exhibition of a more interesting and comprehen- sive character than anything that has been attempted in Warrington before, and that such an exhibition should be promoted and Conducted by local effort. In full sympathy with this suggestion, a number of manufacturers and tradesmen have combined, with the object of pro- rnoting and holding such an exhibition, and for that purpose have secured the Parr Hall and adja- Cent vacant ground for the 'Louth of March. The exhibition will be open from March 3rd to the 24th. _ _

his intendad to devote the Parr Hall to exhibits and entertainments of various kinds, and on the vacant ground to erect a building for the accom- rnolation of machinery and other large and heavy exhibits, and in which the various goods produced in Warrington and district may be seen in the actual process of manufacture by machinery and otherwise. The exhibition will be open daily from 2 until 10 p.m., admission 61., and every effort will be made to ensure success. The comfort and enter- tainment of visitors will be fully considered, and special attractions provided, including promenade concerts by a well-known ladies orchestra, and other high-class entertainments, There will also be cookery lectures held in the afternoons.

The exhibition will be thoroughly representative of the many and various trades and manufactures of the district. It has been well supported by local firms and at the preQont moment there are only a few spaces ta let. The Mayor (Alderman Roberts) has promised to open the exhibition and he will be accompanied by members of the Corporation. Ar- rangements have been made with the railway companies to run excursion trains during the ex- hibition from the surrounding districts. There will be about twenty sections in all. One will be devoted to art, and this will give local artists an oppor unity to display their works for sale or otherwise. In the science section an effort is being made to make arrangements for demonstra- tions in wireless telegraphy. There will also be sec- tions for carriages. cycles. construction,, curios. antiques, decoration, domestic appliances, dress and fashion, electricity, engineering and machinery, food and drugs, furniture, gas, hardware, horticul- ture, recent invention., mining and metallurgy, musical instruments, sanitation and ventilation, smoke abatement, sports and pastimes, sweets and confectionery end other local trades. A limited number of medals are to be presented on the award of k!ompeteut judges. Arrangements have been completed with the Warrington and District Elec- tric Light and Power CampanY, Limited, who have undertaken to put down a plant sufficiently large to supply power and electric light for the whole of the buildings.

The annexo is to be completed by the second week in February.and an entrance will be provided into Parr Hall. There seems every reason to be- lieve that the exhibition will be a centre of great attraction, and it will certainly serve as an adver- tisement for Warrington goods.

.1.14 AMERICAN 'NOVELTY. A lunch basket social, which is an American novelty, will be held in the Mersey Mission on Wednesday next, -and should prove highly interesting and diverting. The idea is for young ladies to provide luncheon baskets " for two"; the baskets are then put up for auction, and the young gentleman successful in purchasing one, will be honoured by the lady owner of the basket par- taking of luncheon with him. The proceeds of the sales, etc., go towards the funds of the mission. The idea will give the young men and maidens a glorious opportuoity of judging whether their tastes agree. A MISSINO RUNCORN MAN. Some four months ago, however. his wife died, and ever since her deeth Mr. Catterail has been very much depressed. He was 5 feet 8 or 9 inches in heieht,of ruddy complexion.with grey hair,whiskers and moustache, and of sou t build. He left home dressed in "Trilby " hat and a long coat. A meeting of the above; Order was held on Saturday night. Mr. Thomas Cooke eccupiei the chair. They now had 289 members, and 119 cadets, who were now worth £lll6s 4d.—Mr. Thomas Clarke, the sick visitor, in giving his repor:7, said that during the past half-year £l3l Hs 81 ha, ben paid to members for accidents and sickness.—Lt the close of the meeting a hearty vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Cooke for his services in the chair during the past twelve months.-1 meeting of the above cadets' section was als) held on Saturday evening under the presidency of Mr. ThomasWhyte. There was a good attendance of members.—The Chairman said that they proposed to have regular meeriags, when they would be instructed in temperance principles; He spoke of theievils ot drunkenness, and urged them to stand by their pledge, and in after years they would become strong temperance men and women. He then called upon Mr. Daniel Bisbrown to give a short address. Mr. Bisbrown referred to the opinion of medical men on the question. and said that drink at the best was unneces- sary, and at the worst it was a fearful danger. He hoped they would grow Up to hate the drink, and become use- ful and good, and thus fulfil the mission of life.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

Several of the younger students who attended the "Students Social" at the Ruuclrn Technical In- stitute, the other week complain" that there was nothing for them." They seem greatly dis-satis- fied, simply because a room was not set apart for them to have" games " in, whilst the seniors were indulging in their " Cinderella." I would suggest, as a rem E dy, that, in future,two nights be set apart for the students—one for the juniors and one for the seniors—in which they be allowed to enj themselves after their own manner. This would obviate overcrowding and conduce to fuller enjoy- ment with greater comfort.

The Runcorn public must be keenly awake! There is to be formulated a scheme for the erection of a " Transporter Bridge" across the Mersey from Runcorn to Widnes, and in connection with it, will come the question of fares. What is ic going to cost the public for min the bridge ? This is the all important question. The public must not be satisfied with the presumption that the Urban District Council will see to that. They presumed that with the old Board of Commissioners at the time of the buil itig of the L. ana N.W. Railway viaduct and footbridge; and well does the working man know what that presumption has cost him. If the "Transporter Bridge" is to be erected with the support of Runcorn, the townspeople must see that the" toll "is advantavous. Otherwise, they must hold out far a bridge adequate for vehicular traffic as well as pedestrian, erected at the expense of the Cheshire and Lsnesshire County Councils. and the Runcorn District, and Widnes Town Coun- cils.

The 'Runcorn Association, as intinaaterl last week, wera making arrangements to secure a consignment of "grown-up trout " with which to stock their waters. But no to the present no grown up trout have arrived. £5 was despatched to the dealer some time ago; but nothing has arrived, and now the Anglers—strangely enough—are much concerned, lest t'le trout are so " grown-up " they wont " land " at all.

THR EXAMINER FRIDAY, JANUARY 12, 1900.

ACTION AGAINST A RUNCORN

FOOTBALLER.

The Trade in Cheap Cigars. Sam Houghton's "Sealed Orders." _

On Monday, at tfa-ta Liverpool County Court, before his Honour Judge Shand, an aozion was beard in which some interesting details were given in regard to what was said to be an increasing demand by the public for cheap cigars. The plaintiff, John Henry Kean. a cigar manufacturer. of Victoria-street. Liverpool, sued Samuel Houghton, licensee of the Nelson Hotel, Runcorn. and a well-known professional football player, for £4B 109, balance of an account for cigars known as the "Sealed Orders" brand. The defendant resisted payment on the grounds that the goods were useless to him on accouat of quality.and that they were not of the descrip- tion or the quality ordered. Mr. Garner Smith (Erwin, Lockett and Co.) appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Rudd for the defendant. The defendant paid £3 23 6d into court. It apoeared that in January last the plaintiff's travel- ler. a Mr Summers, obtained an order from the defend- ant for a quantity of cigars, known as" Sealed Orders," at a price of 81 per box, there being a further stipula- tion that each cigar was to bear a printed label with the defendant's name upon it. A part of the order was de- livered, and the defendant paid in respect of one in- voice a sum of £2 101. Subsequently, however. when plaintiff wrote him, asking him to take deny ley of the balance of the order,he complained of the quality of the cigars, and denied that he had given an order to the extent alleged. The plaintiff, having given evidence as to the facts, was cross-examined by Mr Rudd,and stated that he considered the cigars were of good value as "twopenny smokers." They were, indeed, all that could he expected at the price. He denied that they were fit only to be sold to showmen who gave cigars to successful competitors at Aunt Sally or games of that sort. (Laughter.) The cigars used for such a purpose" were not" twopenny smokers," but" three halfreeny.t Nothing better than the cigars he supplied could be go bathe market at the price. Mr. Robert Frederick Green, secretary of Meesrs. Hirmett Bros , Limited, was examined on behalf of the plaintiff as to the quality of the cigars in question. He gave his opinion that they were worth what the defend- ant had paid, but in cross-examination said it was Use- less to try to assess the value of a cigar at such a low price. Asked if he thought the cigars would smoke, the witness cautiously replied that he thought they ought to burn (laughter.) He certainly would not expect them to be good cigars. Asked further if he would give 41 per 100 for them, he replied that he would not give any- thing at all (leegheer.) Unfortunately, there was a growing demand far cheap cigars. There were so many peoele who bought to sell again, and they all wanted a profit. ks a result, manufacturers had been constantly pushed down in price. In further cross examination. the witness was of opinion that the cigars could not be made, having regard to the cost of the boxes, the labels, and also the duty, for 49 per 100, and he could not imagine anyone expecting anything better for 7s 61 per 100, which was the price of the cigars themselves, the extra sixpence being for the labels. An amusing episode occurred in court through the introduction of a box of Messrs. Hignett's cigars sold at 84 per 100. The inteetion was to examine these, and compare those in question in the action' Various samples were accordingly cut open and examined, but the samples appeared to get considerably confused, and no definite conclusion appeared to be drawn from the experiment. For the defence it was contended that the cigars were altogether below quality, and a Frerch nail, pieces ef wood, and a bit of coat were produced by one witness, who swore he had found them in samples taken from the defendant's consignment. The defendant, on being cal'el, stated that his footbal engagements necessitated his frequent absence irons home, and th,t the business of the hotel was at such times managed by his wife. In his absence she paid for a portion of the goods in question,svhich at that time had not been used. When his customers cemplained about them he sampled two and was not able to smoke them. He never took drink, as he had to "keep himself in con- dition "for football, consequently he sometimes took cigars instead, but he had never tried " Sealed Orders" before, and never wented to again. When he ordered the cigars he thought he was to get the " Dl Kooshas," such as he had got before from the rlaintiff, and he did not know that a box of 50 was known in the trade as half a box. Be introduced the" Sealed Order; "to his customers as a ".new cigar from Liverpool." and those who tried them threw them away. He tried thom him- self and found they were "dead wrong ones." (Laugh- ter.) es a matter of fact he could not give them away. (Renewed laughter.) His Honour held in regard to quantity that defendant ordered beste; containing fifty cigars, and that he had paid sufficient into court to cover the value of the cigars he had accepted. Having regard to what transpired when the order was given, his order was for the came cigars as he had got previously— namely, " Del Kooshaa." He would say nothing as to what his decision would have been if some of the goods had not been used and a payment into court made by the defendant in respect thereof. El's judgment would be for the defendant, with costs.

Resignation of Sergeant Chantler. The Warrington Borough Police Force this week loses a tried and valued member in the person ot Sergeant Chantler,who retired from duty on Thurs- day. after 29 year and 9 months' service. In his well-earned retirement, he carries with him the good wishes of-his many friends. He joined the force on April 14th, 1870. coming from Kingsley near Frodsham. In November, 1880, he obtained the merit badge for good conduct and was appoin- ted warrant and court officer, fifteen months later. In this capacity he came into touch with all classes of people, and gained the esteem and respect of both good and bad. On the 20th of November, 1890, he was promoted to the position of sergeant, and at the Queen's Diamond Jubilee he was selected as the Mayor's sergeant and mace bearer. Recer.tly, he lost his wife, and since then he has been anxious to be relieverl from duty. He resignel on the 11th inst. He will receive a superannuation allowance amounting to two-thirds of his pay.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

The Quality of the Gas. A meeting of this Council was held in the Town Hall, Earlestown on Monday. Mr CB F Barron, J.P., presided and there were also present : Messrs W Coll- ingwood, J W Emmett, J.P., N Siddelev, R Stone, T Wood, J Simpson, Dr. Patterson, W Thompson. W Beatty, J H Forster, E T Evans, T Ormand, with Mr W W Shirley (cferk), Mr A Bowes (surveyor), Mr D H Lloyd !gas engineer). THE CHAIRMAN AND THE NEW YEAR. The Chairman before the business of the meeting was proceeded with said that was the first meeting of the year and he desirsd to wish all a Happy and Prosperous New Year. They were commencing the new year under a cloud but he trusted that the cloud would shortly be lifted. They were fortunately in a prosperenes condition so far as trade was concerned and he hspld that prosperity would continue. (Hear. hear.) He trusted that the fund which had been opened would be well supported in the district so that they might be in a position to relieve the sufferings that would be caused by the war. He had received a letter from Lord Newton which he was requested to bring before the notice of the people in the district. His Lord- ship wrote : " I desire to ask those who are resident in this county or who are otherwise connected with it to subscribe to a fund which it is proposed to raise at once in order to equip in the first instance those officers and men of the Yeomanry and Volunteers from Lancashire who may be accepted by Her Majesty's Government for service in the field in South Africa, and in the second instance to assist the general fund of the Imperial Yeomanry or mounted forces without distinction of counties." Continuing, the Chairman said he believed a number of volunteers from the district were going to join these forces, and it was only natural that the dis- trict should take an interest in their well-being. He should bring the letter before the Committee which bad been organized for raising fields for the benefit of soldiers' orphans and wide ws through the present war. (Hear, hear). CEMETERY COMMITTEE. NOTES BY THE:WAY AT

EARLESTOWN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

At the Council meeting on Monday the Chairman read a letter he had received from Lord Darby in connection with the fund being raised to provide equipments for the Imperial Mounted Force and Volunteers. Seeing that this district is so closely connected with the imperial Mounted Force by reason of a number of the Lancashire Hussars hav- ing offered their services and been accepted for South Africa, there is no doulot that the appeal in aid of the fund will not be in vain. Already the people have subscribed liberally to the fund for the relief of the wives and families of soldiers, and it is not too much to hope that a further effort will be made in the direction of providing a suitable equip- ment for the Volunteers.

The nine or ten Yeomanry from the" C "or New- ton troop had a most enthusiastic send-off on Fri- day night, previous to their departure for South- port and no doubt when they land upon the shores of South Africa they will look back with pleasant memories upon the many good wishes which were showered upon them. Not only do the men take with them the good wishes of their friends and comrades, but they also bear with them practical, evidence of the kindly thoughts of those with whom, as civilians they were associated. One of the most useful presents given them is a plir of powerful field glasses, which according to the evi- dence of those with the Army at the front are in- dispensible articles when oh scout duty.

It would appear from the proceedings of the council that the question of opening up new streets which has been so long under consideration, is in a fair way to be settled. A communication has been received from Lord Newton through his agent that no objection will be raised to the making of a street 40 feet wide. running from Crow-lane acro3s the fields to Wargrave-road. rhis will be a distinct advantage to the township, besides being a great convenience to residents in Wargrave.

===== 0002601_19000112_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL.

The extremely damp condition of the elements on Saturday interfered with the enjoyment of the devotees of the " socket" game. So far as the Earlestown club was concerned, -it was a distinct disappointment. Their fixture was with South Liverpool by whom, it will be remem.bered they were defeated by 4 goals to 2 on Boxing Day,and it was the intention of Earlestown to wipe out this defeat, and at the same time secure a couple of points. The Liverpool men put in an appearance, but they had only nine men, and with this number they took the field, Earlestown having their full strength.

The rain came down pitilessly and within shoot- ing distance of each goal the ground was a veritable sea of mud, rendering correct play an impossibility. The men wallowed about in the mud, and of the two the Earlestown team seemed to enjoy it, The Liverpool men were without spirit, and when three goals were scored against them before half time, they appeared to throw up the sponge and one of their players left the field refusing to continue the game further. And he was wise! At half-time only four of the Liverpool team ventured out, and after consultation it was decided to abandon the , game.

On Saturday it is hoped the condition will be somewhat better, when Earlestown visit Horwich to play their return L3ague fixtutt. Considering the position Rorwich occupy on the League table, and their performances lately, it is safe to assume Rarlestown will be able to give them their quietus and secure another couple of points.

Earlestown Rovers added another win to their unbeaten record on Saturday. They defied the elements and paid a visit to Prescot Athletic in the first round of the Wigan and District Shield, and secured victory by 4 goals to 1.

Newton-le-Willows journeyed to Golborne to PlaV a friendly. but the field was in such a sloppy con- dition that it was band to be utterly unfit for play. Record of clubs to date.

SHOCKING FATALITY AT ST. HELENS GLASSWORKS. At S. Helens Town Hall, on Monday, Mr. S. Brie- house, county coroner, held the inquest on the body of John Macdonald, aged nineteen, 183, Colleee-street, who died on the way to Providence Hospital on Thursday afternoon last after being knoeked down and his head badly emashed by an engine a,: Messrs. Pilxington's sheet glass works. Mr. Erant, her Majesty's inspector, and Mr. J. 0. Swift, solicitor, were present. Two jurymen James Wonlfall and Caleb Jones, failed to put in an ap- pearance. The coroner ascertained that they were both warned on Friday. Mr. Woolfall arrived fifteen ' minutes late, and said he was sorry he had forgotten all abler': the inquest. Mr. Jones did not come, and the coroner fined him 20s, and said he would not remit the fine. Frank Dixon, acting foreman, said the deceased was a labourer for the platelayers. He had to clean the points and assist the platelayers with the lines. Thomas Mooney, 60, Higher Parr-street, braltesrnan, said that his driver was at the weighing- machine, and witness got on to the engine and moved it lower down for some water, When he started witnees saw deceased about eighteen yards away, standing about two yards clear of the line. He was on the opposite aide to the peints. Witness felt the engine go over something, and stopped It. He found deceased underneath. James Radcliffe, r of 37, St. Thomas-street, the driver of the locomotive, I said that in private yards it was a proper thing ler a brakesmen to get on the engine and move it. The jury returned a verdict of" Accidental death."

WARRINGTON.

Personal.

'The Mayor of Warrington" says " Truth," "com- pletely misunderstands his poiition and duty as a magistrate. Because he is strongly in favour of vaccination he seers to think he is justified in bad- gering and brow-beating any conscientious objector to the operation who exercises the right of applying fora certificate of exemption under the Act of 1898, In two recent cases in which the applicants satisfied the other magistrates that they were entitled to cer- tificates the Mayor caused an unseemly wrangle by his perverse opposition, and he wound up by tel- ling one of the men that his objection was due to "stupid iLinorance." His wor,hip ought not to talk of stupid ignorance. He evidently has not yet learned that his business as a magistrate is to ad- minister the law, not to air his own personal views and insult anybody who ventures to differ from him."

Death of Mr. J. M. Porter.

We regret to announce the death of Mr. J. M. Porter, which took place at his residence, 15, Cairo- stre-t,at 2.30 on Tuesday morning. The cause of death was influenzi end failure of the heart's action. The deceased, srlio bad been in business for a consider- able number of years at 22, Horsemarket-street, as a game and peultry dealer, had been attended by Dr. White. Latchford Station Staff Dinner.

The 16 th annual dinuor got up by the staff of the Latchford station arri g 0043 department was held on Friday evening !nst week at the "Cheshire Cheese" Inn. Mr. r r• 1 Mrs. Griffiths. the host and hostess, provided d drat class repast, which was keenly appreciate' by the company of about 25, who were plesnr. After the dinner conviviality was indulged in, ,Er Warren (station master), occu- pying the chair. An excellent • programme was submitted, songs being rendered by Messrs. Fair- hurst, Horton, Bottom, and Woodings ; recitations including "The Absent-minded Beggar," by Mr. Burgess. Mr Alfred Grounds officiteed at the piano and also added much to the enjoyment of the evening by his ventriloquial mimicry and grama- phone selections. The usual loyal and other toasts were honoured, and at the close the chairman, artistes, and host and hostess were thanked for their services.

Prize Distribution at Daresbury. The annual distribution of prizes provided from the Fndowment Fund connected with Daresbury Schools took place on Monday. The following governors were present :—Rev H T Clarke, Messrs. Wynne and Sons, Philip Derbyshire, J Cooper, and H Mawdsley, As usual, substantial money' prizes were awarded for regular attendance, good conduct general efficiency. &c., in addition to books and. presents to those having never been absent once during the year. Those having been present at each. meeting of the school were: Lily Prince. William Arnold, Herbert Leach, Walter Prince, William Jameson, Jenny Roberts, Florence Furnival, Sarah. Ryder, John Taylor, Alfred Potts. Emily Broome. Cyril Furnival, Gladys E Taylor, Garfield Furnival Louisa Calder,end Nellie Warburton,the last-named not having missed a single attendance during the last four years, although under nine years of age. At the conclusion of the distribution three cheers were given by the scholar., who were afterwards dispersed for the usual half-holiday. Prizes were also distributed On the same day at the Infante' schools of Moore, Hatton, and Preston Brook. Death of Mr. Join Hartley. It is with regret we annourice the death at his residence, Sunnyside, Latchford, on Wednesday afternoon. of Mr John Hartley, who for many years was a part proprietor in t'le firm of Messrs. Arting- stall and Co., wire brush manufacturers, Wildeee- pool road. The deceased gentleman who was 67 years of age, had been ailing for about twelve months. On Thursday of last week he became seriously ill, and despite every care and attention never rallied. The primary cause of death was inflaenza. He was attended by Ors White and Smith. The deceased gentleman commenced business life as a clerk in the employ of Mr. Artingstall, and on that gentlemen's death, in 1880, being then cashier, was left a third of the share of the proprietorship. Mr. Warburton, the manager,and Mr. D. Jones, the traveller, shared the remainder. The business was floated as a campeny, and ha since been very suc- cessfully carried on. Mr Hartley retired from active participation in the concerns of business about twelve months ago. He was prominently associated with the Wesleyan denomination. For nearly 30 years he was a Sunday School teacher in connection with the Bold-street Society, both at the old school in Buttermarketestreet and after- wards on the removal of the school to Bold- street. In consequence of failing eealth he gave up the work about two years ego. lie was a trustee of the Bold-street Chapel and schools, and steward and treasurer of the Trost Fund at the Latchford Chapel. In the erection of the chapel at Latchford he took a practical interest, and gave generously to. the building fund. For many years he had worshipped at the Bold-street Chapel, but when the Latchford Chapel was opened he commenced to attend there, and continued to do so until his final illness. He was a most generous supporter of the Wesleyan denomination and took a keen interest in its welfare. He did not take a prominent part in public affairs, but did much ex- cellent work quietly and unestentatiously. In politics he was a Conservative, though at no time a strong party wan. He leaves a widow and daughter. The funeral vill take place to-day (Saturday). There will be a service in the Latchford Chapel at 1.30, and the remains will eubsecineratly be interred. in the Warrington cemetery. LAMPS, Oil Stoves, Coke Stoves, J. S. GARLAND, ' 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.-41dvt.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football.

[By "Oval iSt."ll Warrington have certainly made a promising start in the New Year, for in addition to vanquishing Hull on New Year's Day, they accomplished another smart per- formance on Saturday when they took a weak team to Tyldesley, and won by eleven points to nothing. One expected them to win there under ordinary circum- stances, but the eircum3tancea were by no means ordin- ary on Saturday, and those who feared a different re- sult had ground for their fears. Tyldesley are far from being a strong team now, but with Warrington b e avin: to find substitutes for Bosco w, Smith, Ftsh, Harris, Tay- lor. and Swift the chances looked in favour of the " Lngers "getting a much needed point or two. The weather was all in their favour too, rain falling in tor- rents during the whole of the match.

Warriregton rose to the occasion however, and played a really marvelleus game, everything considered. It is net often they surprise tla.ir supporters in this pleasant fashion, but on Saturday they buckled too with a will and carried all bAore them. Though the ground was terribly heavy and the ball as awkward to handle as it could possibly be, Warrington went in for the passing ,same and with immediate success. The forwards packed well, and the half backs having two good centres be- hind them in Jones and Isherwood, the ball was soon travelling along towards the line. Some really excellent combined efforts were only stopped on the line, and the attack was finally rewarded by a try near the pests. Elea obtained the peint by good following up and smartly profiting by a home forward's mistake. Jones added a goal and then for a time all was plain sailing. Hockenhall put the finish on some clever play by Isher- wood, and Jones completed another concerted effort in fine style. 'I he latter also intercepted a pass and after a brilliant run crossed the Tylleslev line, only to seep over ' the dead ball line in trying to get behind the posts. He was entirely to blame for spoiling what would other- wise have been the feat of the match. At the interval Warrington held the substantial lead of eleven points. The second half was barren if scoring, Tyllesley hav- ing rather the best of matters by dint of rushing forward tactics.

The War,ington men are to be complimented on their smart performance. I dsn't think they ever fielded the ball better, or played a cleverer combined game than on this occasion. The Tyldesley crowd were quite sur- prised at the manner in which the visitor 3 sprinted over the heavy ground and tossed the greasy ball from one to the other. It is difficult to discriminate between the players. Jewkes, who made his first appearance in the premier team played very well, and yet all the other backs were better. Halves and threequarters alike did splendidly. Hallam deserves a word of praise all to him- self however, for he played in tip-top form all through. His display reminded one of those he occasionally gave when he was the regular custodian. The forwa.rda, not- withstanding the absence of good rpen were very strong and active, while their s3rimmaging was far superior to that of the " Bongers."

The win has been the means of helpimz Warrington up in the League table,and we are now once more ahead of Leigh and Widnes. Salford are only 6 point ahead, with a match more and should be easily distanced if the " Wirepullers " only make the most of their chances.

For the remaining three Saturdays of this month War- rington remain at home, and have as visitors, Swinton, Salford, and Runcorn. They are burning to avenge a defeat at the hands of each of these clubs, and I hope they may succeed. The team if fully representative, is strong enough at present to get six points out of these three engagements unless there is some such fiasco as against St. Helens. To-day's visitors never have won at Warrington, though they have been very near doing so, and I hardly fancy they will break the record just yet. Much depends on the constitution of the home team, however, but with Smith and Fish back again thee ought to pull it off. Salford should present litle diffi- culty, and even Runcorn can be beaten if the luck is fairly distributed.

-CRICKET.

William Bates, whose death is reported, was on of the finest all-rouni cricketers of his day. From 1877 to 1887 he was a valuable member of the Yorkshire eleven, and for many years he and Peat e ran a close race for first place in the Yorkshire bowling averages. The best bowling nerformanee Bates ever accomplished was in Australia in January, 1883, when in the course of his first innings of Murdoch's eleven he captured seven wickets for twenty-eight, three of the batsmea falling to successive balls. J. T. Hearne, it will be remembered, Performed the "hat trick" at Leeds last season in an International match, and Spofforth of course gained the same distinction during his remarkable career as a fast bewler. Bates was a batsman of a dashing order, and on his day no other Player could beat him in the rapidity with which he knocked up runs. He was included in several teams sent out to Australia, his last visit being in the season of 1887-8. During that tour he met with a serious accident whilst practising,which had the effect of bringing his career as a fire-class cricketer to a summary close. He subs,quentiv recovered sufficiently to take part in club cricket, and for some time was one of the professionals at Warrington. At the time of his Ideath Bates was only forty-five, and there is little doubt that he sacrificed his life in paying the last tribute of respect to Thewlis, another old Yorkshire cricketer, who was buried last week.

MR. R. H. PEARSON'S CONCERT.

Mr. Pearson's concert announced for Wednesday evening the 24th inst. in the Parr Hall promises to be one of more than ordinary attractiveness. In addition to the reappearance in Warrington of Miss Clara Butt, the famous contralto, which forms the chief feature in the announcement., the opportunity of listening to Mr. William Green the eminent tenor is also held out to local frequenters of high-class concerts. And although public expectations in regard to the last-named artiste have been doomed to dis- appointment on several occasions in the past,it nosy be safely predicted that mans' admirers of Mr. Green's sing'ng will rally to his debut in Warrington, which, it is to be hoped, will not longer be delayed. The soprano and the baritone, Madame Bertha Rossow and Mr. Kennerley Rumford, come to us with the best credentials, and it will be surprising if these gifted singers do not leave behind them a most favourable impres- sion. Miss Clara Butt's excellencies are too well- known as to need expatiating upon, and a hearty welcome doubtless awaits her. As a tribute to her most recent successes it is pleasing to recall that, by command of Her Majesty the Queen, Miss Clara Butt along with Mr.. Scnnerley Rumford had the honour of singing at the concerts given before the Queen and the Royal Family at Osborne so recently as a year ago. At Mr. Pearsoa's concert Miss Butt is announced to sing, by special request, Liddle's "Abide with me '' as sung by her before the sovereign on the auspicious occasion to which we have adverted. Mons. Johannes Wolff, the distin- guished violinist, has already established himself in high favour in our midst, and his reappearance after an absence of several years will be eagerly looked forward to.

THE NEW POLICE BUILDINGS AT NEWTON.

The police court buildings which have been in course of erection at Newton are now nearing completion, and it is expected they will be ready for occupation in about a couple of months hence. It has not yet been decided whether Inspector Moran will take up his residence there,or whether the house will be allotted to one of the sergeants.

He said that Hiton was a bedstead maker and lodged at Airs Forbes's. 6, Church-street. At seven o'clock on Wednesday morning be went out to his work at Messrs Monks, Mill and Company's, leaving the watch and guard with the attached medal safe on the mantel piece in the kitchen. Mrs Forbes saw the articles s if. at 2 o'clock when she IFIt the house, and did not return till five o'clock. On her return she missed the watch ad guard and information was given to the police. luquiries were made and it was found that the articles had been pawned at Mr. Greave's shop, Church-street, by a girl named Maguire- Defendant was subsequently arrested, and the girl identified him as being the man who gave her the things. He asked def-naant's wife for a pound of butter out of that parcel. and she told him it was not butter but margarine. He asked her why it was not labeeled, and she said she had been, cleaning down, and went into the back of the premises and brought a label and put it on. kie asked for a pound and she gave it to him in a piece of ordinary paper without any mark upon it. THEFT PHOM THE CHESHIRE LINES RAlLWAY.—Richard Wettings, 63, Nicholson-street, was charged with stealing, or receiving, knowing it to have been stolen, a quantity of iron chairs, the property of the Cheshire Lines Railway Company, sometime between Tuesday and Thursday last. —Mr Browae appeared for the defendant. —The Chief Constable stated the facts of the case, from which it appeared that the defendant was a green grocer and had a garden and a stable near to tne Cheshire Lines Railway premises at Proghall-lane. On the railway line, there had for some time, been a heap of iron chairs and many of them Lad been missed from time to time. In consequence of this, Instructions were given for the chairs to be piled up in a certain way. and an accurate Idea kept of the amount of iron so that if any were taken they would be readily missed. On Tuesday the heap was seen In its usual condition at four o'clock by the foreman plate- layer named Phillips. The following morning, some of the Iron was missed, and inquiries were made. On Thursday morning two witnesses named Burton and Woodson saw the defendant in his garden breaking something with a hammer. They went and examined it and found he was breaking a num,,er of chairs which were missing from the heap They asked hlm how he got the iron and he said be had bought it from a man, and it had come from there (pointing to the ' railway), and he knew be had done wrong in buying It. If they would give him a bit of time he would put it back where it came from. Information was given to the police and the man was brought to the Police Station by Superinten- dent Monaghan. When charged with receiving the property knowing It to have been stolen he said he bought it for Ss, iron a man on tbe previous night. He knew before he paid for it that it was stolen, and that it belonged to the Railway Company. For the defence Mr. Browne said the defendant was at times subject to loss of memory, light headedness,and pains in his head. Some six months ago he commenced the business of buying scrap Iron. A fortnight ago he took a load to Mr. Forshaw's, Napier-street, and there saw a ma a named Baker. On Tuesday afternoon Baker came to him and asked him if he would buy some scrap irno from him and he said he would. When he came home at night, Baker had brought the iron and it being dark, defendant could not see what kind of iron it was. He gave Baker Ss for it —A fine of 20s and costs Was inflicted.

_ THE THEFT FROM A WAITING Boom. —ln the ease of Eliza- beth Ockleshaw, of Clifton-street, Higher Crumpsall, the Chief Constable said that the woman was before the Bench a fottnight ago on a charge of committing a theft from a wait- ing room at the Central Station. It was then stated that she represented herself to be an attendant and obtained fcom a troy in the employ of a shopkeeper a quantity of lady's wearing apparel to take charge of till the owner came to the station, and made off with the articles. She was remanded for a week, but on the day that she should have appeared he received a communication from the Medical Officer at the gaol to the effect that she was suffering from alcoholism and was unfit to attend. The previous day (Monday) he received a communication of a still paver nature from the Medical Officer, certifying that she was altogether of unsound mind, and was not fit to plead on the charge, so that he (the Chief Constable), had no alternative but to withdraw the charge of larceny. The question of her detention would now be what their worships would have to consider. He would put in the certificate from the gaol and ask them to commit her to the Workhouse as a person of unsound mind, found wander- ing at large.—ln answer to the Chief Constable, the prisoner said she did not know what town she was In.—An order was made for her detention at the Workhouse.

TRADES UNIONISM. Meeting of the Warrington Trades

and Labour Council.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labanr Council was held at the "Waggon and Horses" Inn, Buttermarket-street,on Tuesday evening. Mr. A.C. Binks, the president, presided over a ggod attendance of delegates. THE LATE MB. JEFF BENNION.

The CR&IRBIAN proposed the following resolution "That the members of this Council regret the death of Mr. J. Reunion an old member of this Council, and wish to forward their very great sympathy to his relatives and friends in their sad bereavement. He referred to Mr. Reunion's connection with the Council, and ex- pressed his sorrow at his eteath. Mr. BROWN seconded, and the resolution was unanimously passed. CHEAP TRAINS FOR WORKMEN.

The Secnemax read a letter which he had received from the Lancashire Federation of Trades and Labour Councils, with reference to the unsuitable conditions ' under which workmen travelled. It stated that the efforts of the Cheap Trains Sub-Committee of the Manchester Corporation to obtain more equitable treatmem for the working Clifidf-S had only resuited in an inadequate and, in some cues, trivial reduction in workmen's fares. The Manchester and Salford Trades and Labour Council had intormed the varioug Railway Companies of the dis,atisfaction of the workin4 classes with the meagre 'concessions which were brought into operatien on the 14 July last, and sac;eties atfiliat?d with the Council had passed, and torwarded to the Railway Companies copies of the following resolution:— "That the Railway Companies be and are hereby called upon (a) to revise their late decision and devote to the working classes the prowr share of the funds of which they, under the Cheap Trains Act, 1883, retain posses- sion, and provide workmen's fares on a par with these which ootain in the working class districts of Landon and Glasgow ; and (b) to obviate insufficiency of accommodation which exists in come trains to the discomtert of workmen, :contract ticket holders, and other passengers." Toe CELAIRMLN said the Council had previously passed- resolu; ions on the matter, so it was decided Co take no further action. BAKERS AND THEIR HOURS.—BILL TO BE INTRODUCED IN. PARLIAMENT.

The receipt of a letter I rom the s‘.lcratary of the amal- gamated Union of Cperative Bakers and Confectioners, enclosing a copy of a Bill to be introduced into the. House of Parliament next session by Mr. Lg. Woods. M.P., was reported. The Bill provides that no employe shall work in a bread. biscuit or cake bakery, or in a pastry or confectionery estiblishment more than 48 hours in one week, or more than right hours in any one day, unleAs for the purpose of making a shorter work day on the list day of the week, and that no baker shall commence work earlier than five o'clock in the morning, and work later than eight o'clock in the evening. Toe Union asked the Council for their support. Th. tollowint resolution was unsninsously passed : "This meeting hereby expresses its approval of the forty-eight hours bakehouses Bill to be introducod into the House of Commons in the next Session of Parlia- ment, 1900, by Mr. S. Woods, M.P. Also that copies of the Bill be sent to our local members of Parliament re- questing them to undertake a ballot for a place and to further give it their support in its various stages through the House." LANCASHIRE FEDERATION OF TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCILS.

The PRESIDENT Was unanirnouity elected representa- tive of the Council at the forthclming annual meeting, of the above loderation.

THE FILESMITHS' STRIKE.

On behalf of the Filesmiths' Association,Mr. Battersby the secretary, cordially thanked all who had contributed sums of money towards the maintenance of the men who are at present on strike. Unfortunately at., strike was still progresaing, and had now lastsd six months which was a long tinib for them to hold out. They were still being well supported,. but it was a bitter and a crucial test to them. It took a great deal of money to maintain them so long. They had spent over £3,000 in strike pay since they commenced and they had received over £2,000 from different societies. He thought the fact that they had received that amount showed that the cauqe was a just one, and that trades unionism was not a myth as it had bee represented to be. On Saturday last, two delegates ad. dressed the glass bottle makors oi St. Helens, and a re— solution was passed, grantiug the men £4O, which had been handed over to them. The employers had been approached about four times with a view to settlement bot they refused to lioten to the offers put forward by the men. The Board of Trade had taken action,and the Mayor(Alderman Roberts) h4cl approached the employers on the matter but they took no more noticl of him than anyone else They said they were masters of the situa- tion and would have no interference. The Mayor del all be possibly could and the men wished to thanked him.

On Saturday afternoon the remains of the late Mr. Joseph Swinton,of Mainfield's Farm, Middleton, were interred in the churchyard of St. Oswald's Parish Church. Winwick. The weather was very unfavourahle,and no doubt prevented many friends from being present to pay their last respects to the deceased. The mourners and friends present were Mr Peter Birchall (son-in-law); Masters Frank and Reginald Birehall (grand sons); 4essrs James,Joseph, Thomas and William Roughton (brothers-in-law) ; Mr Smith (brother-in-law) ; Messrs Joseph Hough- ton, jun., Frank Houghton Bennett, and Kirkland Bros. The funeral service was impressively read by the Rev. Canon Penrhyn,rector and rural dean, assisted by the Rev W Williams, curate. The coffin was of lime polished oak, with handsome bras furniture, and on the plate was en- graved the following simple inscription : "Joseph Swinton, born June 15th. 1830, died January 4th, 1900." Several beautiful crosses and wreaths cm- nosed of choice ferns and flowers were contributed by relatives and friends.

Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel.

Mr. W. A. Sandford was the preacher on Sunday morning. In the afternoon the annual consecration service was held, conducted by the Rev. Theodore Bis- hop. The same gentleman preached on Sunday evening, and spoke from the words "And who is willing to conse- crate his servi 395 unto the Lord 11" fie said there were many lazy people in this world—lazy bodily, morally and spiritually. Of all those lazy spiritually were the worst. God's service was a willing one, and there was no compulsion. recruiting sergeant in England did not force youths to join the colours, so it was in God's service. If ministers used conscription there would be a great number of Chrisrians in the world.

Memorial Services at Warrington.

The Late Mr. Finney,

There was a good congregation on Sunday morning at St. Psul'a Church, when a memorial service to the late Mr. D. W. Finney was held. In the course of his ser mon the Rev. E. R. Tarbuck (vicar) made suitable refer- ence to the deceased gentleman. He said that week their congregation had suffered a sad loss, and not their own people only, but the whole town of Warrington mourned to-day the wrenching apart for a time of some of those bonds of earthly Mendable which were se dear to them, and which helped to make life worth living. But three months ago Mr. Finney retired from public life and duty after a long and arduous course of great dignity and usefulness. On his retirement into private life he (the preacher) was particularly struck by the manifold evidences of the respect in which he was held by young and old, rich and poor, in all parts of the town, but particularly by all classes in that parish in which he had worked for the looe. period of 35 years,and he had talked with men who were now middie-aged,but whom as boys at St. Paul's schools Mr. Finney had educated in things both temporal and spiritual. By those and such as those,who might be counted, he supposed by hundreds, Mr- Finney was respected and even revered. Mr. Finney had ,yorked, as headmaster at St. Paul's schools, during a time of very marked progress in the nation 'S views as regarded elementary education, and it stood greatly to his credit, that be himself progressed with this proeress, and in fact helped to encourage on every pos- sible opportunity that public opinion which ruled the actions of evert' department of State, and was also the moral atmosphere in which they lived and which they breathed from day to day. Mr. Finney was always most willing to adopt any new suggestion either from the Education Board or from the managers. Be had only known Mr. Fioney himself for the short space ot nine months, but during that time his respect for him had continually grown, and their mutual relations had been of the most cordial nature. Mr. Finney was emphatic- ally a gentleman and a loyal Churchman, but if there was one trait in his character which seemed to him to stand out more prominently thee others it was his urbanity. He personally felt that he had lost for a time a good friend and well wisher. The sympathy of the whole town went eutto those dear ones whom he had left to mourn his loss,but that sympathy was naturally more deeply felt by the congregation of St. Paul's, among whom he had lived and worshipped and to whom he was always such a pattern of reverence. Might the hand of God be clearly seen in all this. Might they and especially his widow and children,feel that God had but woven another cord around their hearts to join them to His eternal self and to draw them with ever decreas- ing reluctance to the principles of right, of love and of eternal wisdom. Might the comfort of the Holy Spirit up- hold Mr.Finney's family at that time,and might the grace of God be given to them all that they might apply their hearts unto wisdom and so might they end their highest happiness and deepest peace:in the praise of their ever- lasting and ever loving Father, Son, and Holy Ghost. At the close of the service Mr. Wilkes, who presided at the organ, very ofilcieetly played .the Daid March in "Saul."

Wycliffe Church.

At Wycliffe Congregational Church, Bswsey-street. on Sunday evening. the Rev. John Yonge preactied a special eermon in which he made reference to several of the Wycliffe friends who have recently died, taking for his text" Ye sorrow not, even as others who have no hope" (I. Thess. iv. 13), and "I even I, am He that comforteth you" (Isaiah li. 12). In the course of his remarks he said that an unusual number of their people and their families were entering upon this New Year in surroundings of great sorrow. The gloom and shadow that were upon them all, by reason of the national crisis were intensely deepened for these friends by reason oa their own personal and family experience. Surely as community they sympathind with them sincerely an deeply. They would try to enter into their affliction an help them more bravely to bear it by the sense of thoid loving fellow feeling. One of the cardinal Christian graces and the suprernest Christian duties was to enter into the lives and experience of others. " Rejoice with them that do rejoice ; end WM) with them that weep." They grew in true Christian life proportionate!y as they had the heart at leisure from itself. To soothe and sympathize—was not that, indeed the Christian and Christ-like .heart? He was never too busy, too self centred, too self absorbed to notice, and to care for, and to enter into the sorrows of others. He came to minister. "I am among yen as one that serveth." Never was any e'suffering to Him a matter of indifference Never did He withhold His matchless sympathy from these whose lives were troubled or whose hearts were sad. "He went about doing good." He ever gently but firmly rebuked, and removed the fears and griefs of the anxious and the stricken. He thought that all their hearts went out that evening to their young friends —one who had just buried his young wife of two short years, and the other 'alio had. had taken from her side the husband of but six brief months! His heart went out to those who were thus stricken with such grevious loss ! Among those also whom they remembered with regret that night was the man in middle life, the father of a numerous family, who now were left in early orphanhood with neither mother or father to watch over them. Life must look blank and strange to those young hearts, and the world would seem cold unless Christian sympathy could gather round them. Those yoting people had been, and gems still were, echolars in their school. Also there bad passed away from membership in the Church on earth two of their venerable seniors, ono at 71 and the other at 78. Neither of them bad been present with them in that Church for a considerable time, one from having gone to reside in a distant town, and the other from infirmity. Mr. William Owen and Mrs Sarah Boardman had both exceeded the three score years and ten, and now their earthly course was ended and they were passed to the" rest that rernaineth for the people of God." Both of them had lived consistent lives. They lamented over the vacancies that thus were caused. Their departures were losses to them, and they knew that)no oho could ever fill up those vacancieq.Time,with its wondrous faculty of healing power, would deubtless do much to assuaee and reconcile, and so, while they never lost the memory of the &Darted, they would feel less keenly the grief of their 1-:ss. -

Methodism, London and Politics.

Sir,—Pardon me for asking the favour of the inser- tion of this letter in your columns, as it is well known that the Methedist Press is closed against anything that is not in harmony with the opinions of the editors or a few officials whe no more represent Methodism at present than one-tenth of a public meeting in favour of, a given question represents nine-tenths who are against it. Of course. exclusiveness of this kind and the suppression of "the other side " would be considered a disgrace in secular jetrnalism. but in religions journa- lism well—Terhaes you are aware that in Methodism it has been considered an unwritten law for more than one generation that party politics are to be excluded from the pulpit and the Methodist press, but this wholesome rule is now being flagrantly set at naught in the two Metho- dist papers and in the pulpits as well. In last week's " Recorder " there was an able and well-balanced ser- mon from the President of the Conference, and by its side a piece of rigmarole, styled 'a sermon, by Hugh Price Hughes. which revealed a type of mind as if it did not care what the tongue said or did. To say that the latter deliverance was disgraceful and an outrage upon the feelings of the great bulk of Methodist people throughont the country is the mildest way of describ- ing it. The editor of the " Recorder" was equally insulting in his Elitoral Notes. He said : " A -few well meaning people do not understand the signific- ance of this war," etc. Just imagine the sublime im- pudence of a Methodist editor describing nine-tenths of those he should be writing for as a "few well-meaning People." Then he goes on to say that the •tTamm.ns" America are in favour of the Boers. Can it not be said with equal truth that the" Tammany" in England the London music halls, the theatree, the drinking saloons, the gambling fraternity, the street loafers, not forgetting a few editors of the " gospel of peace " news- Tanersotre in favour of this war. If the editor wants to talk of cempanionship,he has a strange lot of heifellows Then with regard to the reporting of H P H's sermon The secular newepapers reported that H.P.H. had been hooted, no doubt owing to the extravagance rf the language used and the feelings of the peopie being so outraged. But in the " Methodist Recorder " this ser- mon Is reported as if there had not been the sliehtest opposition. It is a disgrace I admit to hoot in a place of worship, but it is a far greater (Degrees to the man who provnkes it. London always favours wee-. and the root evil in Methodism is that it is governed from Len- don, and the country is regarded as little better than a great snitch cow to surple London. Compared to its population there is little Methodism in London, but they have always deputations on the wing to get the country to supply them with flreia. Rut es to Methodist papers representing Provincial Methediem there is hardly ever a decent report ex-ent when 9,3T1P man, 'who is "in the London inner circle," is prn.ent. I have heard that one gentleman has prsmised £l,OOO to begin a Methodist newepaner to represent Methodism in the previnces. where it thrives, grows, and supports peiehbouring branches, and where the gag shall not be put upon fel* discussion. I will be responsible for an- other £l,OOO if a suitable body of directors can he found and the paper he kept to Methodist work alone and let the gecular newspapers deal with politics on both sides* We want a newspaper full of Methodist news, not column after column fawn- ing on this man and the other man. I have heard many, not only laymen, but ministers, say they could vomit to see the fullgomeness of these two papers. one mainly on himself, and both nn others whose chief merit was their volubility. A to their power to think, the presi- dent or Mr- Wetkinson would give out as much thought as a gross of these men and not know they had lost any intellectual light.

A NORTHETIN METHODIST.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS.

===== 0002601_19000112_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BATTLE AT LADY-

SMITH. -- Free Staters Placed in

Front.

Expected Quarrel between the Allies.

The Frere correspondent of the "Standard" writes : I hear on good authority that President Kruger sent word to the Boer headquarters asking why Ladysmith had not been attacked. The reply sent back was, "We should lose too In may ma." Mr. K ruger's answer to this excuse was a suggestion that the Free Staters might be put in the forefront. The hint was taken, and the attack delivered accordingly. Mr. Kruger's advice was so far good that his allies behaved better than his own burghers. The Free Staters at leas t managed to seize a hill, while the Transvaalers later in the day retired be- fore General White's counter-attack, amid the jeers of their comrades in arms. The Free State men stuck to their position until they were actually bayoneted in the ditch. After this affair it is almost certain that the allies will quarrel bet wen themselves.

Lord Roberts at Capetown.

Lords Roberts and ~Kitchener, and staff, arrived at Capetown on Wednesday.

Lord Roberts having arrived assumes at once, of course, command of all the forces in South Africa. and in future directs the plan of campaign. It is generally assumed that for the preseat at all events Lord Roberts will remain at Cape Town, whence he is in touch by telegraph with General Builer at Frere Camp, with Lord Methuen at Modder River, and with Generals French and Gatacre in Cape Colony. Too much must not be expected at first from the appearance of Lord Roberts on the scene, as the gallant Irishman haa_immense diffi- culties to face, and is very far indeed removed from the position of a generalissimo starting a campaign upon lines of his own choosing and d evising. Lord Roberts finds himself tied up and committei to certain plans of action, the failure to develop which has for the time being brought the war to a deadlock.

===== 0002601_19000112_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
French Hemming the Boers in.

CAPETOWN, JANUARY 8.

Laffan's correspondent is informed by a personal friend who has just returned here from a visit to Rensburg that General French bolds a very strong position. His troops are hemming the Boers in on thres sides, leaving them only one way of exit. Two thousand more men would enable the British commander to surround them completely. The disaster to the Suffolk Regiment in no way affect's the British position. .

Rumoured Battle on The Tugela.

There was a rumour in the London clubs yesterday afternoon that a battle between Bailer's forces and the Boers is in progress at the Tugela.
