A RUNCORN CIVILIAN WITH THE FORCES.

"A Description of The British Position on The Tugela."

Mrs. W. Cooke, of Waterloo-road. Runcorn, received the following letters frern Mr. John A. Smith, sene and Mr. John A. Smith, jun., (her father and brother), on Monday morning :—" December 8:h, 1899. Luggage van en route to Frere. We left Durban yesterday by the r 5.40 p.m. up mail, and had a pleasant rile to Pieter- maritzsburg. When we arrived (soon after 10 p.m.) the r station was simply swarming with soldiers—sifting,' standing,and lying everywhere—and most of them were having supper. We had a wait of twenty minutes, which enabled us to get some supper, and when we started again we dropped the bunks and went to sleep We reached Estcourt at 3.15 a.m.—pitch dark—and with no signs of any other train to take U 3 on to Frere. Here, as at Pieternaeriteburg,there were lots of soldiers sleeping in all sorts of positions—on tables, seats, chairs, but by far the greater number on the platform.We followed mit, and, rolling ourselves in rues with bags for pillows were soon asleep. I Mr. Smith jun., awoke at 5.30 quite fresh r lend hungry. The Latta: feeling was soon eiipeesed with, r after which I had a walk round the camp, outside the station, and started for Frere about 6 45. About 25 of us are doing this last 15 miles in a luggage van, includ- ing a few officers,soldiers, five civilians and two coolies. The line has been blocked for 50 minutes, and while r waiting I visited a store which had been previously visited by the Boers on a looting expedition. Everything r is completely smashed, and hundreds of empty bottles are strewn about. One of our party killed a sm-.11 puff adder, about two feet long. I hope none of these things pay me a visit when I'm under canvas. Later,we arrived at Frere and beheld a sight I I wish you could see it. There are about 20.000 men encamped round the station. Each regiment is separated. The scene is one of hustle and activity. Here and there are men shoeing horses, some re-packing saddles—then you see a batch of men cooking. This, by the way,is no small thing. Finding and cooking food for 20,000 men gives someone plenty to think about. There are about 96 civilians here, and we are allowed the same rations as Toramy Atkins, which is so far very good. We get fresh meat 11b., bread 11b-, potatoes, tea, sugar, bacon, jam twice a week, &c., Etc., and, what will seem a bit funny to you, 11b. of wood is allowed each man for cooking. This is weighed with as much care as the beef. I, father, and two others have a tent to ourselves and a stretcher each, and are very comfortable. The water is not so nice. It is kbasi colour—river water. Messrs. Lea and Reynolds at home can tell you what river water up country is like. I think it runs over beds of fire brick clay, which colours it. We bathe and wash clothes in the river,then take the water for cooking purposes. One can't afford to be fastidious here, but gets quite used to things like this in South Africa. It is very hot here, it's terrible : and to make it worse for me I lost my big felt hat on the journey up. and have to wear a cap. My nose,ears, and the back of my neck are blistered. Yours, I sup- pose, are nearly frost-bitten. I bought a three-guinea seapshot, and have several pictures which, I hope, will come out all right. After supper I went tor a stroll round some of the camps, and saw several of the chaps I knew in Johannesburg. Saturday, December 9th, 1698. If every night I have to spend under canvas goes off as well as last night I'm all right. The bugle sounds lights out' at 8.30, but as we had a thund, r storm last night our Lgiits were out before that. I slept all night as sound as a bell, thunder, rain, and wind having no effect on me. We breakfast at 8 a.m., and after that I had another walk round with the snapshot. The camp is about early,and after parade the majority have nothing to do, so they pass the time in bathing, visiting other camps, washme clothes, writing, reading, See. Of course some out of each regiment go out scouting in companies of about 150. When I saw Trevillian yesterday he had just re- turned. He had been out since 4 a.m., and it was then 6 p tn. They had been in sight of Coleneo, and had seen a oig number of Boers. They had also seen someone's orchard, for he gave me over a dezen aprieots,which he called ' toot.' All the bilk round are guarded with big guns, and this morning we heard about a dozen shots to the west of us. News in camp is very scarce ; in fact we have to wait for the paper from Maritenuee b,fore we can hear anything. I saw two Boer prisoners brought in this morning, which makes seven we have in camp. Over 4,00$ " Tommies " arrived here this morning, so that we have between 24 and 25 thousand. When the move is to take place I don't know. All the men and, horses here lock in splendid condition. We shall UlOl7B very soon after the soldiers if we don't go with them, so that I am looking forward to being an eye-witness of the next engagement. The men who went out with the armoured train yesterday say the Boers have about 7 miles of earthworks thrown up around Coleneo, so that it will be a stiff fight, but our chaps are splendid fellows, determined to overcoMe anything, and are anxious to be at bueinres I will try and give an account of hew things go. Frere Camp. December 12th, 1899. I am:writing this under difficultiee. Yeti lrn)w in camp life you haven't the conventionalities you have at home. I'm writing sitting on the door with a board for a desk inscribed, " Goeseges." lam afraid 1 sannot give you a descriptien of what life here is like. On all sides waggons are being laden, and all surplus kit is be- ing sent back to Maritzburg. Everything is being put In readiness for a hasty departure, and it is thought that tomorrow a move will be made. There is bound to be a battle this side C lenmo. Every nigor Igo for a stroll to some part of the camp, and I have bad a chat with men of nearly every regiment here. I enjoy going to the camps of the 8.M.1. and T.M.I. which are volun- teer corps, I know so many chars in them from Johanneeburg. I'm not at all sorry now that I failed to get in with them. I shall see and be near everything that happens, and I have much nicer times of it than they have. To-lay there landed 1.200 men from Durban to act as stretcher bearers. It looks like bad business, doesn't it ? Tommy Atkins has already named

===== 0002601_19000112_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, JANUARY 12, 1900

them " Corpse Snatchers" and" Body Snatchers." It does not seem to have had any bad effect on the soldiers, and all are eager to be getting into active service again. I stay out as late as I can. If I succeed in learning the countersign I stay out until I feel ready for bed, otherwise I have to be in bed before "lights out " is sounded. Last night I didn't get the countersign, and I sat talking until it was too late to be out without it, so had to stay in the camp all night. I think it is hotter here than anywhere I have ever been. On Saturday I saw a regiment march into camp from Estcourt. Their kit weighed on an average 90 lbs., and they had only had ene halt on the way. It is about nine miles by read, and they were unable to get any water, They were in a very exhausted state, and when they halted abeut 200 yards from camp, over 20 of the men fainted. The men in camp ran out with their water bottles, and dragged the water cart by hand to them. After a drink and a "cheer "they seemed all right. You've no idea how much good a genuine cheer can do. I believe the cheer on Saturday did as much good as the water. I don't know whether I teld you this before—hut father and I went to gee the wrecked train, and the grave of the men who fell in that encounter. A splendid and original tombstone has been erected. It is built (af concrete, and the letterinte is made by inserting empty cartridge cases. I took a snapehot of both the up-set train and the grave. Frew) Camp, December 15th, 1899

A meat fierce battle has been raging all day. Yester- day morning, about 4 30, we heard the big guns roarine and booming about two hours, then at short intervals during the day. This morning they started in deadly earnest: Cannons were fired for about four hours with only a very few seconds between each shot, then they slackened down. It has been, so far, the fiercest dual between the Boers and the British. The Boer.; have a position which will only be taken at great cost. Our troops have refired after making three unsuccessful attempts to take it. The Dutch are on the north side of the Tugela river,and are most securely entrenches abut 250 yards from the bank. The bridges are destroyed, and as our men try to get to the Boer trenches they are met with a perfect hail of bullete. The crack of rifics sounds like a perfect hailstorm on a tin-roofed roo.n. Our number of killed and wounded is very high. A train load of wounded has just passed. I went in to give some of the noor fellows a drink, and it was a most horrible sight. These are the men who are supposed to have the greatest chance of recovery—men with broken arms and lees, and bullet wounds in many other parts. There were 100 men in this first train. There are a few very bad cases on it. One man I was talking to was shot in the stomach. Another was shot in the neck.the bullet coming out of his side. Several were uncoil- sciona and looked as if they were dying. It was a terrible bloody sight. I will try and write you fully next week. Father and I are both in very good health." A FRODSHAM HIGHLANDER AT MAGERSFONTEIN.

Private Hared Dutton of the 2nd Battalion S.,,aforth Highlarders writes to his parents at Frodsham from Medder River, December 13th 2—" You will be very glad to hear from me, for you will have heard about the brigade being in the battle at Magerefontein. We were on out-poet at Enslin on December 9th when word came for UR to march to Modder River at once. We were re- lieved from the hill at Enslin at midnight, landed at Modder River on Sunday morning—no sleep that night —made an advance at noon on Sunday, then retired and slept till about 2 a.m. on Monday morning when we started again. All went well until about four in the morning. It was very dark and raining. never a murmer from any of the men. Before we knew where we were a volley went right into ue. nem were the Seaforthe. Black Watch, Argyle and Sutherlands, and Flighland Light Infantry. Oh, I will never forget it. We walked right into their (the Boers') trenches ; 5,000 of us against 18,000 to 20,000 Boers ! Someone shouted "Charge with the bayonets !" So we did, but it was no use. We retired. We started firing, and were under fire from four in the morning until four in the afternoon. I was firing from behind a bit af a bush, on my stomach for eleven hours. Every time I moved I was shot at. it was terrible. I got away, dodging from bush to bush, until I was out of range of the Boers' abets. Three of us were behind a bush. A Black Watch man was shot; in the neck. One of our officers crawled to him and bandaged him up. He crept back again, and was just loading again when he was shot in both hands; he got up and ran and he was shot in the leg. So you can guess what it was like. I have leo: a lot of my chums. We had eeven officers killed, 200 men killed, wounded, and missing. So I thank God lam safe. My chum from Manchester was wounded. They have sent him to Capetown. It is awful to see them in the hospital. We were burnt some- thing awful about the legs, and we dare not stir, or we got a bullet at us. Oh,it was ter. ible ! The Boers were firing on our hospital waggon. I shall have something te tell you when I see you." FIFTY RUNCORN VOLUNTEERS SIGNED FOR THE FRONT.

Another successful concert with the object of swell- in e the funds for the relief of soldiers and sailors families and widows was given under the auspices of the Ron- corn Detachment of the 2nd Earl Cheshire Volunteer Battalion, at their spacious Drill Hall, Greenway-road, Runcorn, last Saturday night, when the chair was occupied by Captain George F. Ashton. The programme opened with " Gems of Viet erian Melodies "a tuneful selection given in the band's best style. Master Jack Rutter gave" The pardon came too late " and other favourites, being accorded a hearty reception for each. Miss Carter's rendition of "A. Soldier and a Man" caught on remarkably and the audience joined in the refrain. Her second item" Massa's in the cold, cold ground" was equally as fascinating, and for both she was loudly fn. cored. The quartette party Misses Jc nee and Birkett and Messrs Morris and Birkett gave " Carni- vel'a," "On the Banks of Allan Water " and " Now the day's retiring lamp." Roberts' rendition of "The Soldiers of the Queen" was perhaps the most popular item of the evening. The whole concluded with "God Save the Queen" by the band. Mr. A. C. Timmins proved an effioient accom- panist.—The Chairman in the course of a short address delivered during the interval thanked the artistes for their hearty co-operation, and especially Mr. Temperley who had kindly placed a piano at their disposal for the whole of the series of concerts free of charge. He also apologised for the absence of Miss Coward, whose brother he was sorry to hear had only that day suc- cumbed to a sudden illness, and for Mr. Harrison who though down to sing could not do so on account of a severe cold. Before concluding, Captain Ashton appealed to the young men of Runcorn to join the Volunteer Force, and pointed out that perhaos in the past certain young men might have looked upon the volunteer movement as ptaying at soldiers. It was not so ; for the present war had shown that the Govern- ment were appreciating the offer of volunteers for ser- vice at the front. Over 50.0ut of 150 of the Runcorn Detachment had signed their names as ready for the front. No doubt that number would not be required at the present time.but they were ready when called upon. (Hear, hear.) He urged all young men to come forward as volunteers, and so save the country from the other- wise ce-tam, though hateful conscription. (Applause). MILITIA ABSENTEES AT WIDNES.

At the Widnes Police Court on Monday before the Mayor (Alderman J I Neil), and Alderman J W Wareing. John Lancaster was charged with being an abseratee without leave from the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment of Militia. P.C. Davidson said that he met the prisoner in Victoria-roar! on Saturday. When prisoner saw him he turned round and ran lway, Wit- ness gave chase and found him hiding in a house in Victoria-street. Pr.s)ner had no pass. In reply to Mr. Lewin (magistrates' clerk) prisoner said they would not give him A P‘S3, so he left. He was going back.— Stephen 'McDermott, Peter Hogan, and Martin Breen were also charged with the same offence. They all told a similar tale—Alderm in Wareing said it was extremely probable and he symoathisecl with them, but it was a breach of discipline. Breen's sister said she wrote for her brother to come over as her father was bad. A friend of Lancaster said the men had volunteered to fight for their Queen and country, and it was very hard for them to be locked up for coming to see their friends. The Mayor said he had sympathy with them, and they regretted that it was against discipline for them to have left without a pass. They did not inteed under the cir- cumstances, to grant the constables a reward, so the men would not be punished. They would have to remand them to await an escort,as they had to adminis- ter the law, and he hoped the men would do their duty when they got back. THE MILITIA AND VOLUNTEER OFFICE RS' EXAMINATION. Railway Station by the 8 28 train for Preston, to join the other men of the regiment who left the town last week, and where they are being prepared for active service in South Africa. The men left Orford Barrack i shortly after six o'clock, and, headed by the Depo: bugle band, marehed to the station, where they were cheered by a large crowd. About 800 men of the battalion have now left the town en route tor the Cape.

A procession was then formed to the Town Hall, the band playing "God bless the Prince at Wales" and other pieces

At the Town Hall the escort wan received by the Mayer, in his robes, and attended by the sword and mace bearers, the members of the Corporation, magis- tratea, the Board of Guardians, and other townsmen. The procession was then reformed and proceeded to the Parish Chu, ch in the following order :—The Volunteer Band led the way, and immediately behind came Lieutenants Lyon and Vaughan bearing the colours. Then followed the escort, members of the local Volun- teer Battalion,the Warringten corps of tbe St. John Am- bulance Brigade, under the direction of the Chief Superintendent (Captain Waring) ; borough police force, under the direction of the Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot), the Mace Bearer and Sword Bearers, the Mayor, with the Town Clerk on his right and his Chaplain, the Rev. John Yonge on his left, members ef the Town Council and officials, magistrates, members of the Board nf Guardians, and the general public. Amongst those present were:—AlOcrman Wright (deputy Mayor), Alderman Faircleugh and Alderman Hutchin son ; Councillors F R Roberts, J Shaw dreenePlumpton, Peacock, Smethurst, A J Jolley, A Jolley, Tiionion, Smethurst, John Evans, James Evans, Lee, Bennett, Shillcsck, Dr Guest Gornall, Messrs T Lougdin (borough surveyor), J Sutcliffe (assistant borough surveyor), John Fairhurst (borough treasurer), J D 3,1.4 (seater engineer), W S Haddock (gas engineer). G F F White (deputy magistrates' clerk), E Place (market inspector), W H Wallingtoe (assistant),' R Nor- man (nuisance inspector), T Birchsll, J.P., j W Hay- ware, J.P., S Wright, J.P., James Hepherd, J.P., Mrs Edelsten, Messrs Ashcroft, G Pilling, T B Malkin, F Auk:and, W,W Winstanley, E V Walker, G Roberts, R M Rob erts. Henry Wilson, Thomas May, Whittle, R T Fairclough, Clegg, E Lunt, H Taylor, M Knowles, ' F Pendlebury Large crowds lined the streets all the way, and much interest was manifesto( in the ceremony. In the church was the depor contingent of the King's Own Liverpool Regiment, under Captain Bailey. The escort was met at the ehurch door by the clergy and choir, and the colours were deposited in the chancel. The Rev Canon Willis occupied the pulpit, and delivered an impressive sermon from the first and last verses of the 46th Psalm. He said he would ask them to try and realise the serious and critical character of the present situation, and see what reasons they had for deep thankfulness to God. He supposed that by that time they all knew South Africa almost as well as their own country. In almost every house they might find a map, and their eyes were fixed upon the theatre of the war. They could picture to themselves their troops ranged round three sides of the Orange Free States-.-.the east, the south and the west, and north of these lines was a hostile army, and south a disaffected population. Three of their forces were be- sieged at different centres—Mafeking, Kimber- ley, and Ladysmith. Along the line of the Tugela lay most formidable entrenchments, an they were waiting with anxiety as to what would be the next move of Sir Recivers Buller. The country,they knew ,favoured the movements of that hos- tile army. We were deficient still in cavalry and in artillery, and the attitude of foreign powers was not exactly friendly, although we trusted it might be so. The issues of the war were great, and we were standing upon our trial, our supremacy being at stake. And yet we had cause for thankfulness. There was a growing acknowledgment that God was with us, and that God was our refuge and strength.We did not realise this three months ago. We sent out our first troops with rather a light heart, with warm congratulations and expecting them soon to return. We did not realize all that was before us. Now we did, and we realized the import- ance, nay, the solemnity, of the issues. What was the meaning of that service that day ? What was the mean- ing of that congregation ? What was the meaning of IP) 'ceremony if 1-, be not that we realized that God was with us and that we were looking to Him for protection and help ? Again, what reason ss e had to thank God for the attachment of the colonies, for the affection, which was believed to be real and deep, of Canada and Australia, and, though, perhaps, we hardly expected it, of India tco—circurnstancss so different to Canada and Australia. The colonials were our own blood relations, but it was not so in India, and yet they were shrinking from the thought of black or dark-coloured nations fighting with white men. We cuuld 'not be too thankful for that feeling of attachment on the part of our colonies to the mother country of England. What reason had we not for thankfulness to God for the courage and devotion of our soldiers, sailors, military volunteers—the splendid courage and devotion of all who were taking part in the war,from the Commander-in-Chief downwards. He had nearly reached the allotted span of life, three score years and ten. He had made a name and reputation throughout the world, and yet he was willing to risk it again at the call of his country and go out and lead our troops. What did we not owe to our sailors, so anxious to leave their ships to fight on land for Queen and coun- try ? But what should he say to the fifty representa- tives present of the thousand belonging to the battalion who had volunteered for service at the Cape ? What should he say to the 10men of the Warrington Volunteer 11Corps who were going out to the Cape also. Had we not reason to thank God for the courage and for the dovotion of these men, particularly of the militia and volunteers, who were leaving their quiet homes and their pursuits and livelihood at the call of duty ? Had we not reaeon to thank God for the sympathy and for the generosity of the people ? Whose hearts did not go out anel who had not tried to show their thankfulnesa by deeds ? At the present morneet we read that cur own relief fuud had reached the large sum of £3.000 and that of the Lord Mayor of London £500,000. Had we not reason to thank God for all these things ? Ho wished, in conclusion, to address his words more par- ticularly to the soldiers and to the militia and voluntetrs. He would elk them to fix their eyes, if they could see them, upon the colours in the chancel. He wanted them to think what those colours meant. For the last 200 years he understood, from the time of Queen Ann, every battalion of tht Line and the Militia and Volunteers had two colours--the royal colour and the regimental colour. The royal colour was the Queen's colour, and it was composed of three crosses surmounted by a crown and a lion. What did those three crosses signify ? There was the red cross of St. George, the white cross of St. Andrew, and the red cross of St. eatrick. The red cross of St. George lying upon the white cross of St. Andrew marked the union, just 300 years completed, of Eng:and and Scotland, and the red cross of St. Patrick lying upoe the white cross of St. Andrew marked the union, just 100 years com- pleted, of Ireland ; so that the three crosses, the white cross of St. Andrew, the red cross of St. Patrick, and overlying them the red cross of St. George, marked the union of England Ir 4anel and Scotland. And so when they looked at them and thought of them they saw three things,—the lion to teach courage, the crown loyalty, and the three crosses devotion. That was the meaning of tt e Queen's colour ; and the regimental colour I recorded the deeds of the regiment in the past. These colours were now to be deposited with the Mayor for safe custody until the msa crane back to England, God grant them all a safe return. When they were in that foreign land let them try to have consideration tor these who were for the time only their foes, and remem- ber that 400 years ago the Portuguese owned the coun- try. Try to think of them. They discovered the Cape. One hundred and fifty years afeerwards the Ditch took the country, and for 150 years they had held it, while for chi, last 100 years we had possessed it. It seemed as if God had given it into our bands for high reasons—not for cur own solfinh gain, but to spread a wise and good government throughout the land, and to teach the reli- gion of the Lord Jesus Christ. I ask you when you are , away to show deep consideration for those who will be but for a time your enemies, and that we shall be one again. During the service special prayers were read, and the hymns, " Fie,ht the good fight," " 0 God oesr help in ages past," and "Onward Christian soldiers" were susg At the close the National Anthem was rendered.

Outside the church the proceetioa re-formed, anloece mere wended its way to the Town Hall. Here, Ctp ale Skirrlwson behalf of Colonel R. Ireland Blackburne com- manding, gave the colours into the charge of his Wor- ship, The Meroa said :—Officers. N.C.O.'s, and men of the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment. I receive withimuch pleasure through you, acting on behalf of your commanding officer, Lieutenant-Colonel R. Ireland -Blackburn(' whnsA nanyl has been long and honourably asenciated with this town and district, the celaurs of your battalion. I gladly avail myself of the present op• rportunity of joining with my fellow townspeople, in ex- pressing to to you our warm admiration of the motives which have prompted you to show your loyalty to our i beloved Queen, and rour determination to assist in the ' nreservat ion ef her vast empire by volunteering for an- tive service in South Africa. I fervently pray that it may please the Great Ruler of the Universe so to order events that Ihe present war may be brought to a speedy and suceessful issue, and that you may all return t ) your native land in safety bringing with you those laurels of victory to which your devoted patriotism so fully entitles you. While the colours remain in my poss,sion, they shall be faithfully cared for, and I shall look forward with joyful anticipation to the day when they shall be restored to their lawful custediane,to have emblazoned noon their folds air victories which you have helped to win. The colours were then conveyed to the Council C bar whore whore they are to be hung, and the proceedings terminated. The escort were prwithd with dinner at Orford Barracks,after which they wore conducted to thr. station by the Volunteer bugle band. They received a hearty send-off from the Large crowds assembled. MAJOR BUCKTON SUGGESTS 1 LUXURIES FOR THE MILITIAMEN. The Mayor has received the follwing letter : Bewsey New Hall, Warrington, Bth January, 1900. Dear Mr. Mayor,— _

Allow me to draw your attention to the announce- ment in to-day's papers that the 3rd Battalion Prince or Wales's Volunteers (seuth Lancashire Regiment) will emb irk in the City of Rome next Monday for South Africa. That battalion, formerly the 4th Royal Lanca- shire Militia, is one of the oldest institutions of Warring- ton. At their annual training for many years the men were billeted in the town, the headquarters and perman- ent staff were in the B trracks at Rank Quay, and the officers' M 033 was in a,id-street. They still asssrnble arid obtain their equipment here every year. The fact that they gave their colours into your charge yesterday shows that they look back to Warrington as their home, and the only proper place in which to deposit the most precious treasures that they possess, the emblems of their duty, loyalty ,and honour. I venture to assert that this feeling towards the town is fully appreciated and reciprocated, that we are proud of the Warrington Militia, and especially proud of the way in which all ranks have ccme forward now, beyond their strict duty, and have offered Uric services at the seat of war in a colony where the Militia cannot be compelled to serve. ' I hope that you think with me that the town would like to show this in some definite form, and, therefore, I suggest th tt it should do so by presenting a supply of tobacco, literature, Sze., for the use of the men during the voyage, and some cigars for the officers. There is very little time available, but if you would make such a proposal publicly, a sufficient sum might soon be raised for the purpose, and perhaps for the provision of other comforts. Tobacco could be put on board the ship from bond, duty free. Yours faithfully, J. D. BIICKTON. His Worship the Maven. of Warrington. THE DATE OF DEPARTURE.

It was announced on S.iturday that three transports have been taken up for the conveyance to South Africa of seven battalions of Militia, estimated to number in all 5,303 officers and men, who have been ordered for war service. The City of Rome starting from Liverpool, pro- bably, but not positively next Monday (the 15th), will convey the 2rd south Lancashire, 824 strong, from Preston, and the 4th Argyll and Sutherland Highlanders (Royal Renfrew), 828, from Dublin. Twenty six field officers' horses go ow, with the battalions but no machine guns.

PREPARATIONS FOR VOLUNTEERS AT WARRINGTON BARRACKS.

The authorities at Orford Barracks are shortly to re- ceive three companies of Lancashire Volunteers,who are to be prepared for embarkation to South Africa. A company of 116 non-commissioned cfficers and men will be made up of the let and 2nd Prince of Wales Volun- teers from Warringt m and St. Helena respectively. This company will strengthen the let South Lancashire Regi- ment now forming part of Sir Charles Warren's Brigade in Natal. A second company will be composed exclu- sively of the sth Irish Volunteer Battalion from Liver- pool, and this will be attached to the Royal Irish Regi- ment, which has just landed at Durban. A third com- pany of volunteers is destined to reinforce the let King's Liverpool Regiment, at present in beleagured Lady- smith. This company will be composed of volunteers in several Liverpool corps. The date of departure has not yet been ordered.

THE COLOURS OF THE SOUTH LANCASHIRE MILITIA.

lrnpressiie Ceremony at Warrington.

Sunday last will be remembered for many yeas to come as witnessing one of the most interesting and unique events in the history of Warrington, for ou that day the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, which has volunte-.,red for active service in South Africa. placed its colours, those emblems so dear to every British-soldier, in the safe keeping of the people of Warrington, as represented by the Mayor, Alderman Roberts. The ceremony was a striking and impressive one. Each battalion pos- sesses two colours, the Royal and the regimental,and it son the latter that the honours gained by the regiment are inscribed. The regimental colour of the South Lancashire Militia as yet bears no record of glorious deeds, though it is noteworthy that this is the second time that the battalion has volunteered for active service, the first occasion being during the Crimean War. Its services were not then re- quired, but no that they are, the battalion may be trusted, if it has the chance, to gain distinction, and to put the name of some hard-fought South African field on its colour. The banner is cf white silk, with a red cross on it. In the centre are the Prince ot Wales's feathers, surrounded by a circle of thistles, inside which are the words, "The Prince of Wales's Volunteers." rhe number of the battalion is in the left-band corner, in Roman numerals. The Queen's colour is familiar to all, and does not need describing. Th,, escort chosen to convoy the colours to Warring- ton, which is the depot of the regiment, assemblPal on tile square in front of the officers' quarters At Fulmsod Barracks at a quarter to eight. Captain Skirrow was in command, the other officers being Lieutenant Lyon (a nephew of Mrs. Lyon, of App'eton Hall), and Vaughan (who carried the emblems) and Lieuten int Carson. The rank and file numbered 40 and the non-commissioned officers 9. The party left the Barracks for the station at 8 o'clock. Warrington was reached about 10.30. Out- side the station a guard of honour was in waiting com- posed of the officers, non-c.ornmissioned officers and men of the Volunteer Battalion who had volunteered for active service in South Africa. Thee were in command of Colonel Ridgway. Major and Adkltant Ronnie, Major Buckton, Captains Edelsten, Appleton, J D Fairclounh, and Lieutenants Croafield and Willcocks. They axoro joirlecl be the Fire Brigade,under the direction of Super-. mtenderA Turner ; and -then the escort left the stet.ion the Volunteer Band (under the direction of My. Arm 1 strong) played the first bars of " G3d Favo the Queen.'

At the examination in Practical Music held by the above Society at the Royal Institution, Colquitt- street, Liverpool, December 11th, 1899, the follow- ing pupils of Mrs E Latimer, A.R.C.0., (Earlestown) were successful : —Pianoforte playing, Intermediate Grade (pass), Mr Colin McHardy ; Elementary Grade (honours), MiSA M Blything ; Elementary Grade (pass), Miss Kelsall ; Preliminary Grade (pass), Miss F Bowes ; Preliminary Grade (pass), Miss M Apple!_on ; Preliminary Grade (pass), Miss M Lyons; Preliminary Grade, Singing (honours), Miss Edith Sirett. iNQUEST AT EARLESTOWN.

The inquiry into the circumstances attending the death of Patrick Concannon, briefly reported last week, was opened in the Town Hall, Earles- town on Friday morning by Mr. S. Brighouse, Coroner. Mr. ttiley, solicitor. St. Helens appeared on behalf of ks.3rs. Parnell and Son, contractors, Rugby for whom decea,ed had worked. After cer- tain evidence had been taken the jury proceeded to visit the spot were the accident was said to have happened, and the inquiry was afterwards adjourned in order tnat a post-mortem examination might; he made.—The inquest was resumed on Tues- day morning by Mr. Brighouse, who, as a preliminary complained of the comfortless condition of the room owing to no fire having been kindled. He sent for the Hall keeper, who in reply to questions said that the police had only given him very short notice and he had not had time to put a fire in. The Coroner said if that was the way he was to be treated he had better revert to the public house where he did at least get c omfort. Dr. Valentine on being called stated that the deceased was broaght to his surgery on the morning of October 13, having met with an acci- dent at his work, a brick falling on him from a height of 22 feet on to his head. Witness examined him, and found he was suffering from a very severe scalp wound, with violent concussion of the brain. After five weeks' regular attention deceased par- tially recovered. He was then attended at his sur- gery up to a week previous to January 3. On De- cember 29 he was taken suddenly ill with bronchial pneumonia and violent delirium, and died on Janu- ary 3. He had made a post-mortem examination of the body, at which Dr. Latham assisted. Re attri- buted the immediate cause of death to bronchial pneumonia.—ln reply to the Coroner witness said he thought the accident might have left deceased in a weak state, but both he and Dr. Latham agreed that death was due to natural causes.—After some consideration the jury returned a verdict of "Death from natural causes."

NEWTON:YEOMANRY AND VOLUNTEERS FOR SOUTH AFRICA.

On Friday evening a smoking concert was held at the Conservative'tub, L'arlestown, in honour of the members of the Newton troop of the Lanca- shire Yeomanry fluesarsoind of the `• G" (Newton) Company of the Ist S L Prince of Wales Volunteers, who have offered their services for South Africa, and have been accepted. Mr. John Robinson presided and besides the Yeomanry and Volunteers, there was an exceptionally large atten4ance of well- wishers. The Chairman in a pithy speech stated the object of the gathering which he said was to wish God-speed to toO3e who had given their services in their country's good, and to assist in upholding the honour of the empire. fie was sure they had the heartiest wishes ot all present for their safe return from the seat of war, and he had no doubt they would do regimenttthoewirhibeehstoteoyubpentoonldgetdh.e(chhoeenroB7.Thoef ctohne. cart then proceeded and the following programme was very efficiently rendered. Glee, - "Sailor's Chorus "; song," Off t,,s the Front," Mr G Wright; recitation, " Charge of the Light Brigade," Mr W Sutton: humorous, "The Lily of Laguna," Mr J Huddleston ; glee " Comrades in Arms " ; song, "Soldiers of the Queen," Mr Cll Lea; song, The British Lion," Mr W H Kay ; recitation, "The Absent-Minded Beggar," Mr W Sutton; song, "The Young Brigade," Mr C H Lea; humorous song, "All for the sake of Finnegan Mr Huddleston ; song, " Red, White and Blue," Mr George Wright ; Glee, "Soldiers Choras."—The following t 08,48 were given at intervals in the concert : "The " C" troop "of Lancashire Hussars, responded to by Sergeant-Msjor Perce, end ex-Sergeant-Major Strange. "The Volunteers," responded to by Captain Dr. Patterson, and Sergeant-Major Killeen. At the close votes of thanks were proposed to the artistes by Mr. Owbridge, seconded by Mr. G.Moon. After the concert, Mr. John Robinson, on behalf of the Colonel Wareing lodge of Orangemen presented Sergeant Owbridge of the Hussars, with a pair of powerful binocular field glasses, expressing the hope that they might prove useful to him in dis- covering the whereabouts of the enemy. The pro- ceedings throughout were most enthusiastic and on siaturday morning the Yeomanry left by train for Southport to undergo 10 days' training before proceeding to the front. LOCAL BANKS AND THEIR DIVIDENDS. The directors of the Manchester aril Liverpool Dis- trict Banking Cmspany report that the gross profit for the half year ending the 30th Delember last, after pro- viding for all bad dents, is £200,893 its 31., to which has to be adied the amount brought forward frana the 30th June last, £24,020 144 11, making a total of £224,914 5s 3i. The net profit is £144,903 94 61. The directors re- commend a dividend of 15i a share and a bonus of 59 a share, both free from income taT. It is propoied ta carry to the reserve fund £20,000, and to not acc.vunt £24,903 Os fid. The directors whose term of office expires on the 17th inst. are Mews William Jones, John Frederick Knott, George Lewis, and John Parks. They are eligible and are recommended for re-election. The directors of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Bank, Limited, report that, as will be seen from the minted accounts, the net profits for the year ended 30th Decem- ber, 1899, after payment of all expenses and making provision for all had and doubtful debts and for income tax,amount to £90,036 1s 91. which,with £6,165 134 4d, brought forward from the previous year, makes a total of £96,251 15s id. The board are of opinion that an increase may now be mode in the dividend, and they, therefore,recommend in addition to the interim dividend of 12s per share paid in Jaly, a further dividend of 15s rer share for the half year ending the 30th December, 1399 (making a tot 41 distribution of 13i per cent. for the year, clear of income tax), adding £5.000 to reserve fund (raising it to £390,000), and carrying forward £10,251 15i id. The atnaleamation of the Adelphi Bank, Limited, annouoced to the shareholders by circular on the 13th January, 1899, has been completed, and in- creasec the paid-up capital of the bank to £600,000.
